At Her Brightest and Best.

How Miss Nan Stuart e

Among the fortunate few who have won success early in their stage careers, Miss Nan Stuart, the charming light opera artiste, may regard lir.trTelf as included. Yet it would have been surprising if such talents as this young lady possesses had remained unrecognised, for she is an actress as well as a singer. Playgoers will remember Miss Stnart's appear- ance as " Alice" in " Dick Whittington " at the Lyceum, also as " Lisbeth" in" The Love Mills " at the Globe; since then she has performed in vaude- ville and will shortly take a leading part in a new musical play at the Lyric. In an interview recently the fact was disclosed that constant study has affected Miss Stuart's health considerably at times. "You can understand," Miss Stuart explained, "that having to stay away from the theatre is enough to set my nerves on edge; what absences I have had to endure have usually been due to attacks of Nervous Prostration, arising from giving every thought to a new part. I always knew what was in store for me at such times, for dreadful headaches seized me first of all : then I was fit for nothing." " And too anxious and excited to take proper meals or rest ? "

"Exactly,' was the reply. "How can one eat in such a state of nervous diitress ? I used to starve rather than take food, and yet that only made my health worse. As for resting or obtaining proper sleep, that was impossible. So, as was only natural, I lost a good deal of strength. I thought very seriously of giving up the stage for a time in order to recuperate. Fortunately, that did not become necessary." ".In what way, then, did you overcome this bar against your progress?"

WILLIAMS

THE DAYLIGHT SAYING BILL.

STATEMENT OF PROGRESS TO JULY, 1912.

The Daylight Saving Bill was introduced into the House of Commons in 1908. It was referred to a Select Committee, who examined 41 witnesses, and reported that the effect of the Bill would be (inter alia) : To facilitate the training of the Territorial Forces; To benefit the physique, general health, and wel- fare of all classes of the community ; To reduce the industrial, commercial and domestic expenditure on artificial light. That the object of the Bill is desirable and would benefit the community if it can be generally attained and that the weight of the evidence submitted to the Committee agrees with and supports the view ; and That the: nron,sed object cannot be attained without legislation. The Bill was ie-introduced by Mr. T. W. Dobson, M.P., in 1909, when the second reading was carried by 130 to 94, and another Select Committee was appointed to hear evidence from certain interests which asserted that they would be adversely affected. The only opposition of any importance came from : (a) The Great Eastern and the South-eastern and Chatham Railways. (b) Some agriculturists. lc) Some members of the theatrical profession. (d) The committees of the London Stock Exchange and the Liverpool Cotton Exchange. AS to (a). The opposition of the Great Eastern and South-eastern and Chatham Companies was limited totheir continental traffic, a matter of relatively small importance, having regard to the fact that out of a total of 1,259 million passengers carried in 1907, only I', million were continental passengers, and of these Some were carried by the London, Brighton and South Coast Railway and the London and South- western Railway, both of whom support the Bill. As to (c) Possibly fewer people would attend in-door entertainments. However, Mr. Hayden-Coffin, the well-known actor, gave evidence in favour of the

Bill, and Mr. C. AmMiCruster, Musical Adviser to the London County Council, said :—" I am satisfied that the attendance of the public to hear our bands would be almost doubled. Thus more value would be obtained for the money spent, and while peorle are enjoying the fresh air and listening to the music, they are kept from public-houses and similar places." As to (d.) The American Market of the Stock Exchange may be prejudicially affected for five mouths in the year. This difficulty could be overcome by Stockbrokers and Cottonbrokers transactin* American business, working during the period mentioned until 5 o'clock instead of leaving off at 4 o'clock. Sir H Babington Smith K.C.B. (Secretary of the Post Office), on behalf of the Postmaster-General stated that only the Continental night mails (repre- senting G per cent. of the total handled by the Post Office) would be affected, and that the remaining 94 per cent. could be dealt with without difficulty. The present Postmaster General voted in favour of the Bill, and Sir Alexander King, K.C.8., the present Secretary of the Post Office supports it. Resolutions in favour of the Bill have been passed by 4 County Councils and 100 City , Corporations and Town Councils (among the ;Corporations are the Cities of London. Liverpool and Manchester, and of 23 out of the 2S Borooghs which form the County of London, and some of the leading cities in Scotland, Ireland and Wales.) By the Convention of the Royal Burghs of Scotland (representing 199 Towns) and By the Conference of Urban District Councils of England and Wales in July, 1909, when over 200 Councils were represented. The Bill is also supported by 113 Chambers of Commerce, and by the Associated Chambers of Com- merce in London in 1909,1910, 1911 and 1912, over 100 Chambers being represented on each occasion. These last mentioned resolutions supply conclusive evidence of the fact that the business world attaches no importance to any fear that inconvenience to important interests would attend the operation of a Daylight Saving Bill. Public meetings, presided over by the Lord Mayor have been held at the Guildhall, London, in 1009, 1011, and March, 191 f, at each of which about 1,800 persons were present, and resolutions were passed approving the Bill, and requesting the Government to

11111

PINK PILLS.

grant facilities for its consideration by the House of Commons.

The Bill has also received the approval of 43 Trade Unions, and 47 branches of the Shop Assistants' Union, and of over 400 business, political and otber associations and societies, golf, rifle, cricket, bowling and other clubs.

Similar Bills have been introduced into the Parlia- ments of Canada, New Zealand, Victoria (Australia), New South Wales. and Newfoundland. The Canadian Bill was referred to a Select Com- mittee, who, after taking evidence, reported in favour of the Bill.

' The New Zealand Bill has been considered by a Select Committee, who reported in favour of the Bill The Select Committee appointed by the Parliament of Victoria, after examining 62 witnesses reported in favour of the Bill and recommended its adoption throughout the Commonwealth, adding that " if the other (Australian) States should not agree to adopt the Bill, the advantages arising from its adoption in this State (Victoria), would so greatly outweigh any disadvantages " that they recommended "that it should become law in Victoria," as they were "con- vinced that it would give their industrial population such an advantage that the other States would neces- sarily have to fall into line." In New South Wales a Parliamentary Select Com- mittee has been appointed, and has commenced to hear evidence.

In South Australia, in July, 1911, a deputation waited upon the Premier with reference to a Daylight Bill for that State. The Premier said he was in favour of such a Bill, and would endeavcur to secure its introduction as a Government measure. A recent Conference of the State Premiers of the Australian Commonwealth recommended the Bill to the further investigation and consideration of the State Governments.

In Newfoundland, a Bill, having passed the Upper House of the Legislative Council, was sent down to the Lower House, where it was leferred to a Select Committee, who have not yet issued a report. In the Cities of Fort William, Port Arthur, Ontario, the princirle of the Bill has been in operation for the past three years, standard time being advanced one hour in May and put back in October. All objections have been forgotten, and everyone is agreed upon the great benefits gained during the summer months, when people are able to continue their canoeing, boating, baseball or gardening, as the case may be, for another hour. The proposal has also aroused considerable interest in France and Germany, and in the latter country a great number of influential gentlemen, including a number of members of the Reichstag, favour the intro- duction of a similar Bill.

In the following paragraph a Runcorn man tells of his good fortune. We not only offer him our con- gratulations, but thank him for his outspoken state- ment. Many of us, too, will profit by his experience. Mr. J. White, who resides at 3, Arthur-street, Run- corn, says :—" It was owing to a cold I caught, which affected my kidneys, that first caused my backache trouble " he says, "I can tell you that for some weeks I suffered agonies both at work and at home. The pains would come over me whenever I stooped or had to lift anything, and after I got warm in bed at night—there was a thick sandy sediment in the water too. "Just about that time I heard of Doan's backache kidney pills, and I sent for a box to see what they would do for me. I am thankful I tried them, for they proved to be just the medicine I wanted. I got relief as soon as I commenced to use them, and after giving them a fair trial, I was as right as rain again, and I have kept well since that time. The kidneys are perfectly healthy, and my back does not pain me in the least. I shall always be glad to recommend Doan's pills, and you are quite at liberty to publish this account of my experience. PRISON FOR NEGLIGENT PARENTS. At Runcorn, on Monday, George Morrison, and Mary, his wife, of Surrey-street, were charged, at the instance of the N.S.P.C.C., with child neglect. It was stated that there were six children, the eldest being aged ten. One girl bad a severe form of skin disease, and two of the younger were cripples through rickets produced by neglect and improper feeding. LALL RIGHTS RZSERVED..I COUSIN SARAH , PILKINGTON.

BY R. MURRAY GILCHRIST,

Author of "Passion the Plaything," "A Peed- , land Faggot." " Nicholas and Mary," "Lords and Ladies," "The Gentle Thespians," &e.

Josiah Belk, throughout his sixty-five years, had borne the repute of being the kindest and most amiable creature within u twenty miles' march of Grassbrook. 'To be sure, luck had favoured him; he had inherited the compact little farm from an uncle before attaining his majority, arid had, moreover, a very comfortable Xttle property at the nether end of the village. His good fortune had not, as It might have done with any other, young map, turned him int 9 a drone; he bad worked complacently till his forty-fifth year, then had married and enjoyed a twelvemonth of easy happiness before being left a widower with a daughter just three days old. On tho evening after young Yob Hancock had come with word that he wished to take Susan to wife, the old man was filled With utter dismay. Since his daughter had been able to toddie about she had ministered to him with a very pretty devotion, and now, in her early woman- hood, seemed so indispensable that the thought of any change turned him quite sick and faint. His churchwarden pipe brought no solace, nor did the quart jug of good home-brewed that-ho had fetched from the cool cellar. He sat before the fire in the parlour gazing moodily into the flames; now and then tears ran down his plump 'cheeks—tears because of the pain self-interest must cause him to inflict Amon 'his darling. ' A patriarchal gaffer, white-haired and rotund, 'with .a 'beautiful beard that 'reached to the bot- tom 'button Of his waiStcoat. He was handseme still, with .a fine-bridged nose. and a little red mouth; his 'eyes were pale blue, and somewhat protuberant.; silver spectacles had been pushed high on his 'brow. In the matter of clothing he had always been very fastidious; he wore now a suit of deeply-corded velveteen and a pair of gay slippers worked by Susan's hands. At six o'clock his daughter, who had been spending the day at the market town, returned, and after leaving the horse and trap with the lad, came straight to the parlour, and bendir,g over him, kissed his crown, where the hair was beginning to grow thin.

"I'm back,- father!" she said. "I'd a grand time in Bakewell. . See what I've brought you." , She dropped a large packet of tobacco into his hand; to her surprise he let it fall unheeded to the floor. "Ay, I see you're back," was all he said.

Her arm went around his neck. "Why, father, whatever's gone 'wrong?" she said. "I'm sure something is amiss!"

Before replying he fumbled for a red-and-yel- low pockethandkerchief, and carefully wiped his eyes. "All the world's amiss," ho said a broken voice. "I'm sure I don't care whether I live or die!"

"Oh dear! oh dear! what can have hap- pened? Are you ill, father dear? Tell me, we'll have old doctor up at once." He pushed her away with considerable petu- lance. "I'm ill in the mind," be said plain. • "and there isn't a doctor living as can cure that. I feel its I did afore your poor mother was took!"

The girl's rich colour had faded; her lips were slightly parted, discovering her even white teeth. "Why don't you tell me what's wrong?" she said. "If 'tis ought I can help." He burst into undisguised sobbing, and bent so low in his chair that his beard covered his ponderous knees. "And I've been such a 'good father to you," he moaned. "I've guardea you like to the apple of my eye. Nought was too good for you; you've been used like to a lady born. A kinder parent ne'er wore shoe leather. .. . And to think as you should lead Yeb Hancock on se as he came this very afternoon and asked my leave to wed you... . That sly you've been. 0-0--h, dearie me! 'tis more nor I can bear!" Poor Susan's hands rose to her heart. -"bit, father, he's been coming to see me for the last five years--"

"f thought 'twas to see me, Susan. or I'd no'er have encouraged him. He's a rascal, that he is, pretending as he liked my company, and talking about politics and market prices! But I've sent him away with a sore ear, by Jowks! reo warrant you he'll none poke his nose here again in a hurry!"

- "What did you say to him? Oh! father, /do trust---"

"You may be sure as I said nought unbe- coming. 'Thas always been my way to bridle the tongue. I just up and told him as he only fancied as you cared for him, and as you'd got to spend your time witli me One man's enough for any lass to tend, egad! And I said as I'd been sere mistaken in his intentions: I'd thought he'd come here for a talk and a pipe: but, choose how, he need no'er trouble himself again!"

"But I'd look after you all the same, father; Shepherd's Flat's only half a mile away; I'd come over every morn. And there's Aunt Emma Vickers; she'd be only too glad to manago the house." Josiah sat upright in his chair. "No Aunt Emma Vickers here, thank ye!" he said coldly. "You're my daughter, and you'll do as I bid. I doubt if I shall live another twenty year; when I'm gone you can do as you've a mind. Now just you take off your things and help the wench to Niok supper. I could fancy a -bit .of that cold pork."

Susan, with a heartrending sigh, left the par- lour and went slowly up the steep staircase that rose from the square lobby. Before she had reached the landing, however, she turned dizzy, swooned for the first time in her life, and fell heavily to the bottom. Josiah rushed out with a cry of alarm, and found her lying unconscious on the stone flags. He lifted her easily—in spite of his stoutness he was very strong—and bore her to a settle. The servant, a gaunt woman with a hare-lip, came gaping to his side. "Mercy upon us all!" groaned the terrifici old man. " I do believe as she's broken her arm. Give me the brandy, Libby; 'tis in the corner cupboard; then send Vs illiam a-horseback for old doctor."

In another minute Susan opened her eyes and strove to rise, but fell back with a sharp ex- clamation of pain. " Is't hurting bad, my pretty?" said Josiah. "Heigh me, whate'er must we do? Libby, whate'er must we do?"

"I'm sure I don't know," replied the simple woman. " ne'er seed such a job afore. I reckon her arm 'll have to come off! My Uncle Slack, he broke his, and lay two month in hos- pital, and when he came out, why, he'd got a limb o' wood, with a hook at the end!'? _ . .

Josiah covered his face. "Eh, poor Susan!" he faltered. "And her so young!, The girl had fainted again; to his dim eyes it seemed as if she were really at the point of death. Re- covering himself, he dipped his handkerchief in water and bathed her white face. The servant gave was to her emotion, and wept so loudly that he was obliged to command silence. ''Twill be an hour or more afore old doctor 'tomes," she sobbed; "and here she's passing away as quick as quick can be! And no 'neigh- bours in tho house; nob'dy to help straighten Der out !"

Her words excited the old man to a degree of panic.; "You'd hest run off to Shepherd's Flat and bid Mrs. Hancock come at once. None a minute must you lose; it maybe as she'll know how to manage! " The woman waited for nothing snore, but rushed from the house and made ii short cut across the fields, being in so desperate a; hurry that afterwards she recollected nothing but a wild scrambling over limestonh walls. In less time than Josiah had believed possible, she re- turned with Yeb's mother, a stout and comely matron of fifty, who had not even tarried to remove her lappeted evening cap. She' Met the old man somewhat angrily, brushing him aside with a certain contempt. "I doubt you've been up to some mischief, Mr. Belk," she said. "If I hadn't been vastly fond of Susan I'd ne'er have come, after the way you've used my lad. Nay. I always respected you till he told me, but now .. well! For good sake do you clear out of my road. If I'm to do Susan any good I can't have vou buzzing about. There, look at her, poor thing, she's coming to! 'Twould be best if her eyes didn't light on you for a while!" He stared at her in great dismay. "I thought ' this was my house," he began. Mrs. Han Cock, however, laid a hand (M each shoulder, and .pushed him. through the doorway. Now stay outside till you're called." she said. "Things are in a bad way, but you'll make 'em worse. you will! " Then, as he retired in a dazed fashion, she knelt beside the settle, helped

re' c.aght her skirt as she moved away. "Mrs. Hancock Mrs. Hancock for mercy's sake you'll bide here this night? Libby 'ld be no manner of use with a sick person, as you'll know without being told. And as for me, well, I've ne'er had any nursing to do in my life." "Doctor he says as happen you'd like a person from Bakewell; but, to be sure, he thought if only I'd bring my mind to stay, 'twould ho wisest—seeing as some of those young misses with hospital training need a couple of servants to wait on 'em. And Susan, poor wench, she begged me to bide with her till she gets about again." "And you promised?" cried the gaffer. "Well, 'tis vastly good of you, and I hope I'll do the same to you some day." "Come POW, you don't know what you're saying" she observed, zeprovingly. "Go and do as I bid you, and I'll see to the drink." "You'll let me look at my poor lass? I couldn't close my eyes—" "Oh, that tender heart of thine! Nay, Mr. Belk. old doctor's given her a sleeping -draught, and says not by no means must she be disturbed." She took his hand, and, without any further resistance, slowly helped him down the stairs, shook the cushions of his easy-chair. and then hastened to the kitchen, where she prepared a hot spiced concoction of great potency. After he had drunk this with considerable gusto he grew drowsy, and retired to his own chamber. whence in a few minutes came the sound of heavy snoring. As the clock struck ten Mrs. Hancock, who had frequently drawn aside the window curtain, opened the outer door and stood in th-, porch. The whole valley was full moonlight ; along the ridges of barren moor pale clouds drifted to the east. In a short while she passed through the garden, and reached the gate just as a stalwart lad turned the corner of the lane. "I couldn't help coning, mother, when you didn't get back" he said. hope—l hope my poor lass is none seriously bad?" "None she," replied Mrs. Hancock. "Acci- dents will happen; and, as I've told her, it might have b.-,en worse. Ten stairs there are— 'tis a wonder she only broke her arm instead of her nook. Old doctor says with care she'll be right as a trivet in another month." "Thank Heaven for that!" he exclaimed. "Has she—has she said ought about me, mother?" "None yet, Yeb; there's been no chance; but I'm sure she will in the morning. You go to bed and sleep your worries off—l'm tending to everything hero now." "Ay, but she'll ne'er mate with me against 'her father's will. She's too good a daughter for that." "Happen you're right," said 'his mother. "All the same, if I've a grain of wit left in my headpiece, I dare lay my soul as she'll call nr) mother! Lawk-a-me, .do you hear yon noise— 'tis Mr. Belk a-snoring hearty as can be. I made a posset for the poor soul, and put half-a. tumbler of brandy in. I'd brought your grand- father's flask, thinking as they mightn't have any in the house. Ho was fretting sorely; I scarce e'er saw a man in such a case. It grieved me, it did. I used to think as he were lapped up in selfishness, but he does feel about poor Susan." "So you're going to bide all night, mother? I do hope you'll be home afore•noon." "I'm none going to leave her till she's up and about," said Mrs. Hancock, obstinately. "There's no particular call for me at home just now, and I'll trust you to see as all goes right. You an tell Emma to pack my best clothes and ,send 'ern down." " Well, it does seem to me as you've gotten Something on your mind." "Happen so; but, if I have, I'm none going to tell it. Now, lad, you'd best be going. I'm off indoors. Hays you a message for Susan?" "Tell her as I'm sorry to the heart; but toughtql change roe till I die, mother." She put her hand on hie breast and kissed him with more tenderness than she had shewn for years. "Now, don't come till I send word," she said. "Good-night, Yeb, and Heaven bless You, my lad." She stood watching him out of sight, then returned slowly to the house. The gaffer's snoring was louder than ever; its sound brought much perplexity to her simple soul. ." Egad," , she muttered. "I hope to goodness he doesn't make such music every night!" Libby had laid a supper-tray in the pa,flour; the good dame ate , heartily of the cold pork: drank, being an ab- stemious creature, a small glass of nettle beer, and went up to Susan's chaniler, where she made herself very comfortable on the settle which had been brought from below. She did not sleep, however, but lay pondering carefully upon the upshot of the next few days. Soon after dawn, seeing that Susan showed signs of waking, she went downstairs and prepared a light break- fast of tea and toast. The girl, smiled palely when s.ie reappeared bearing the food. "You are kind to me," she said. "I really wonder how you could care to come after the way, father used Yeb." "Don't worry, my lass," said Mrs. Hancock.. "Sup some tea and eat a bit—you must be fair starviiim Then we can talk matte ns over You're

"nat-tlieSsea, oecKoning toner eaeger.:y.

"How's my poor daughter?" he said. "I'm going in now to look at her." The dame moved towards him sharply. "And that you're none," she said. "Susan's given me orders as you've to keep away. I daren't let you in—she's feverish, and belike it might go to the brain. She's set as set can be on none seeing you." She could scarce help laughing at the old man's grotesque dismay. "I'm vastly scared se you've done her a mischief," she continued, re- ,ntlessly. "'Tis odds but she'll turn out like kjousin Sarah Pilkington."

" Cousin Sarah Pilkington " he repeated, numbly. " You'll none remember her, seeing as she lived in another part of the country. But her mother set foot on a chap's courtship, and Cousin Sarah ne'ec- forgave her. From being the gentlest of her sex, siv_• became the most masterful; and poor aunt lived a hog's life for % good twenty year!" • t. over Lome with emotion, went back into his chamber. Mrs. Hancock continued, her progress downstairs, her forehead wrinkled with unaccustomed perplexity. "Parson says the end sometimes justifies the means," she mur- mured. " 'Tis true, I had no cousin called Sarah, but he'll ne'er know. And now I've got- ten to play my game in earnest, for Yeb and Susan's sake."

S • r • • •

It is of little importance whether or not Mrs. Hancock had foreseen from the first the satisfac- tory conclusion of her stay under Mr. Belk's roof. She persisted in her declaration that the old man should not see his daughter; and, dur- ing the third afternoon even allowed him to listen to a long conversation, in which she pleaded, with apparent earnestness; and Sarah's very strenuously (as her father thought) pro- tested that her feeling for him was altogether changed. Never again in this world—the words were her own—could she tend him as she had tended him in the past! The good woman strove to atone for the girl's obduracy by ministering very cheerfully to his creature comforts; and before the fifth day had so environed him with content that he had almost forgotten poor Susan's existence. What appealed most to him was a dish of puddings, superior to any found in the best Bakewell shop. On the seventh even- ing Susan left her chamber for the first time, and slowly made her way to the parlour. The door hung ajar; through the opening, now to her surprise, she saw her father and Mrs. Hancock sitting very close together near the hearth. The gaffer was nodding and winking roguishly; the dame's head was turned aside. " Well, in course it shall be as you will," said Mr. Belk. "Your farm's as good as mine, and belike Yeb'll rather stay there. Susan'll none mind changing, that I dO know. . . 'Twould be best if we went to church wi'out all Grassbrook talking. seeing as we're on in years." I'm with you there," replied Mrs. Hancock. "Ay, I'll come to this spot: us women fancy to change. And now I reckon as I'd best send for the led to tell him things is settled right. Eh! but Pm thankful as you'll have no Sarah Pilkington to keep house with you!" She caught a glimpse of Sarah's whisking skirt, and left the parlour just in time to catch her on the third stair. "I don't know, love," she whispered. after a kind ki”, "I, don't know as I haven't growed fond of the old chap!"

[THE END.]

-- What woman can honestly say that she is entiiely happy ? THE HAY CROP IN CANADA. •

Although wheat is popularly regarded as the great asset of Canada's farming lands, and the chief source of Canada's future wealth, it will come as a surprise that wheat does not hold the first place, even at the present moment. It appears tt at hay, ordinary common bay, is the most valuable field crop at present raised in Canada. In 1911 the hay crop was worth 321 millions sterling, while wheat came second at 274 millions sterl- ing, and oats third at 25i millions sterling. The orovince of Ontario produces !one-third of Canada's hay crop, namely, nearly 54 million tons, or at I'7l tons per acre, valued at 11 millions sterling. It is not suffi- ciently recognised that Canada from the Atlantic to the Pacific is pre-eminently a dairyman's country where cattle thrive and where mixed farming forms the best guarantee for the future of the i country. The present concentration on wheat growing n the West is merely incidental to the opening up of new territory, and in due course more intelligent vuethods of farming will prevail, adding st:ibility to agriculture and assured prosperity to the population.

DOC FOR AWARDED DAMAGES.

A claim for £5O damages was brought at Wakefield on Tuesday agaiust the Yorkshire Electric Tramways Company by Mr Benjamin Holroyd Slater, medical officer to the Bradford Guardians. Whilst riding a motor-bicycle between Glass Houghton and Pontefract on May 3rd, Mr Slater, owing to a hole in the road, was thrown and his machine damaged. He himself was slightly injured, and was prevented from attending his patient& It was argued in defence that plaintiff shotdd have seen the hole. Judgment was given for plaintiff for £35 with costs.

THE CYCLIST'S NOTE-BOOK.

[l3y " Silver Street."]

A TOUR IN CORK AND KERRY.. V.

One of the many suggestions offered gratis since the loss of the "Titanic " as safeguards was that which should require the companies to run their liners in pairs. It may be a surprtse to some to know that this idea is already carried out in these Irish roads, in the ease of motor char-a-banca. The roads are only laid on turf moss, for miles, and although they served very well for the old coaches, a very different state of affairs has arisen with the advent of these great vehicles weighing nearly three tons empty and carrying 36 passengers. Ode sank hopelessly in the gutter eight miles from Kenmate, on our first Thurs- day night, and its duplicate rescued the passengers and took them on to Killarney. At the Suspension Bridge the passsengers all alight and walk over. The vehicles make an awful mews of these once good main roads. That is why the second-class roads in Ireland come first. Our way home led through Glengariff and Bantry. The rocky sides of the road are dotted everywhere with London Pride or King's Feather, f our English gardens—merely a weed there. A rise of 10 miles brings us through three short tunnels to the summit, 983 ft. where a tunnel of 200 yards long pierces the rock, and saves a great climb, but it is a messy place. The road metal is not rolled,,anci it is almost unposible to ride in it, although lighted

prepared for a6l miles ride to Cork. The character of the country had completely changed, the road follows the Cork Bandon and South Coast Railway almost the whole way, but that by no means spoils it, nor is the country uninteresting ; it is a land of small holdings. We stayed a few minutes at one cottage for refreshment and talked with an old age pensioner. "Lloyd George !" said he," I would give ohm a shtool wid foive legs on, and the last should be a good ' un." These new farm cottages are very well arranged. In the kitchen where we had our glass of milk,—the peasants seemed to scorn to give you water, and" we doan't charge nuthen fur milk "—a little fan is let into the concrete floor, and with a large wheel in the corner the peat fire may be blown into new life, and the kettle boiling in a moment or two. But the people bitterly resent all these gifts. They are most independent in spirit, and now we had left the tourist country we could see how title are Goldsmith's lines:

_ "But a bold peasantry, their country's pride When once destroyed. can never be supplied. The land is nowhere half employed, so great is the need for the young folk. At Dunmanway a horse fair was in progress, and from there the run down the Bandon valley through Ballyneen and Bandon was charming, though the finest scenery, at Downdaniel Castle and Innishannon (what swtet names Ireland has, for its people and places!) was reserved to the end, just as we left for the uplands above Cork.

lii the centre of Cork in the Patriot's Monument. It shows Erin's love for England. At the four corners are statues of the leaders in the great

===== 0002601_19120706_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
MAIN STREET, BANTRY,

dimly from either end, and by a single shaft in the middle. One morning, as we were in the middle one of the great motor cars came through, and it was a squeeze to keep out of harm's way. But once on the Glengai iff side you feel inclined to throw your cap in the air and shout. I defy any Englishman to describe the first sight of Glengariff Bay. It is acknowledged the finest view in these Islands of Britain, and the 7 Miles ride is unsurpassed in everything except mere road surface.

None of the mountains seen from here exceed 1900 feet, and yet their pointed summits and the depth of the valleys make them appear giants. Rounding the shoulder of Barraboy we actually doubled from south- east to north-east for over a mile so as to get round and down into the valley. Looking back at the rocky height round which we doubled, it seemed as if nothing but an aeroplane could have bright us down there. And then a wood of dreamy quiet led us to Gleugariff. The wild Atlantic waves must be delighted to come to rest in that lovely haven; the wonder that they ever wish to get out again. The finest site is Eccles' Hotel where the yacht owners , stay. Just across the road from the front door is the salt sea, with seaweed covered rocks, and islanda innumerable. Is there a more beautiful spot for a ' house in Britain? Yes, I believe we found it that day right at the head of Bantry Bay. It is Ballylicky House; fancy such a, name; and if any reader has ever seen such a sheet of salt water fifty yards from a house under the edge of the lawn with wood and garden, swans, skiffs, summer-houses covered with fuchsias and escalonia as we saw that day on that high road to Bantry, I would like to hear of and visit it. I don't believe it exists. _

. . Bantry is bad and beautiful—l hope our editor has room tn insert my photo of Bantry main street as we saw it—and the road surface for a mile or two is dreadful.

" Where every prospect pleases," And only main roads are vile.

We stayed the night at Ballycarrick's Hotel. That name is fictitious, but gentle reader we would spare you that last horror of Bantry, so we rose early and

rebellions, and slabs of marble contain the names of those who have lost their lives in these great struggles with the "sister " country. No ; English rule there ti impossible.

Cork looked much better in the bright sunshine, and our return to Liverpool was quite devoid of incident.

WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB.

Thirty-one members rode to Frodsham, Five Cross's, Kingsley, and Pickering Bridge on Saturday. The four who motored brought with them an excellent gramophone, whose tones sounded well by the river side, and so helped to pass a deligh,tful evening. Mrs. Wilkinson had to accommodate us at two" sittings- down," and so, what with one thing and another the walk to the Devil's Garden was cut out of the pro- gramme, and a long and charming ride substituted, through Frandley and Antrobus, by lanes lined with ferns, foxgloves, valerian, honeysuckle„and•wild roses.

Lamp-time to-night 9.40, next Saturday 9.37. WARRINGTON WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB.

Day run to Bostock Hall, Saturday, June 29th Fourteen started at 930 from Victoria-square, Stockton Heath, and a quick run was made to Daven- ham via Weaverham. Whilst at dinner there a very heavy fall of rain showed what the riders had escaped. Walking the two miles to the entrance, past gardens everywhere crowded with roses, Colonel France- Hayhurst's park was reached at 2.30, and the gardens, greenhouses and aviaries received our first attention. Then the boa.: races by members of the Northwich 8.0. Two really good entertainments by pierrots were enjoyed, and in the game of "Hoop-la" the hon. treasurer won a Coronation mug. On coming out of the park. we were pleased to see seven more members who rode with us home, reaching Stockton Heath about 10 o'clock.

PILLS

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

FOR

All roads seemed to lead to the Hippodrome on Monday evening, when there were crowded houses for the presentation of the new "bill," which is well up to the accepted standard in Barbauld-street. The Bell-Hattons present a fine spectacular and musical programme, which is replete with gems from the operas, There are six instrumentalists who all possess a marked proficiency with that somewhat uncouth instrument, the saxophone. Thos. It. Bell submits a saxophone solo with great skill, and there is no lack of hearty applause when the turn is over. Syd Walker, comedian, in his famous role of the debt collector, is a great draw. His characterisation is reminiscent of Dickens in its drollery, and with the assistance of two or three humorous tenants and debtors to call on, Walker presents a scene crammed full of fun. Cycling turns seem to be quite the rage, and following the dainty lady who graced the wheel last week, we now have the Berg Brothers, showing what can be done with the " freak " cycle and a large stock of daring and ingenuity. The brothers are well in the forefront of " variety " cyclists, and possess a sense of humour which helps to make the wheels go round. The Three Clarks specialise in club-throwing, club- swinging, and ball-punching the latter an accom- plishment in which we can never see much to admire. But it suits the sporting " gods " right enough. The Clarks are very dexterous as jugglers, and the lady member of the troupe dances prettily. Vona Valma shows enterprise and versatility as the musical comedy maid, her songs in character forming attractive reminders of "The Dairymaids," "Miss Rook of Holland," &c. Belle and Austin present a sketch entitled "A Fatal Rehearsal," which involves a large output of energy on the part of the two psrformers, and provokes considerable laughter. A bright juvenile troupe is presented by Madame Thompson Wood. "Daisy and her Eight Stars" twinkle brightly in song and dance both individually and collectively. The Palace Perfection pictures complete a capital " bill." At the conclusion of the perform- ance on Monday evening the whole of the artistes assembled on the stage and joined with the audience in the singing of the National Anthem—a recognition of the Royal Command performance taking place the same evening at the Palace, London. MR. WIDDOP'S SUCCESSOR.

Mr. E. A. Widdop, who has delighted many Warrington audiences by his able conductorship of the Palace orchestra, has been appointed conductor at the Palace Theatre, Halifax. His successor, Mr. Charlesworth, A.C.V., London, wielded the baon for the first time here on Monday, and should soon prove a great favourite. Mr. Charlesworth comes from the Palace Theatre, Bath, and is a late leader of the Hall 6 Orchestra, Manchester.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ANGLING ASSOCIATION. (N.A. CENTRE).

During last week end, many anglers were out for two or three days and the set board has been defaced on more than one occasion. Llangollen, Chester, Hartford, Mickle Trafford, Norton, Lincoln Broads and Ireland have been visited by members, but apart from the specimens weighed in sport has not been plentiful. The members doing the best are Messrs Bryers, Haslem, Morton, Singleton, Hewitt and Robinson. Those who visited Llangollen on Tuesday last, alhough catching the river with plenty of water and in good condition, found the sport entirely off. A few dace were tte only fish caught amongst half-a-dozen members. CHESTER.

The Dee and canal were both fished, and the canal had the best of it. .1 Bryers, who fished the river only, had two bites during his stay, but he was rewarded by a catch each time. They were two fine bream, one draw- ing 21135. 14oz. lldrms., and the other 11b. 12oz, 10drms. These are the finest bream in this season.

HARTFORD.

The Weaver was entireli-oif.---§everal members who visited here came home on the Saturday after arranging to stay until Monday evening. They had quite enough with the two days.

THE GOW Y.

Here was a blank day as far as specimens go, and there was a lot said about giving it a rest for a while and trying the canal across the Shaker Bridge. Members again returned before they had had their allotted time.

NORTON.

The canal did fairly - well - here, Mr T Singleton weighing in a fine fed roach drawing llozs. 11 drms, and it was beautifully clean. William and Walter Hewitt both did fairly well. They had roach ranging from 60zs. up to 10ozs. and had fair bags. .1 Robinson also had a fair catch and be returned with a nice bag of roach. Leas i

If human skill and energy can prevail, the Howley footbridge will be opened in time for the Old English Fair on Thursday next. But the help of one of Grimm's genii with a magic wand would be greatly appreciated. * * There are improving prospects of a lake in Victoria Park. The Borough Surveyor has prepared a plan, and the Town Clerk has been instructed to approach the Ellesmere trustees with a view to the extension of the lake on land belonging to them.

0 * The prospect of a visit to Warrington by King George in July next is daily improving If our bridge-builders could only put forth super-human efforts during the next twelve months they might provide the one induce- ment which would clinch matters with Royalty.

* - Mr. W. A. Redmond, MP., and other Irish delegates sent to Australia to collect funds for the Home Rule cause, have concluded their tour through New South Wales, where £7,000 has been received by them. Reuter's Sydney correspondent says the total for the whole of Australia is £30,000.

* * The Junior Conservatives who tried to impress the dwellers in Bewsey Ward with the evils of Home Rule had a lively time on Tuesday evening—in fact the meeting was a complete fiasco, the speakers after a vain attempt to induce the audience to take them seriously, quitting the field to the accom- paniment of derisive laughter. * *

* - The drastic steps necessitated by the out- break of foot-and-mouth disease threw a large amount of work upon the officials of the Warrington Health Department who had to trace and examine all Irish cattle recently imported into the district. Fortunately Warrington was found to be entirely free from the much-dreaded disease. However, the inclusion of the town in the area scheduled by the Board of Agriculture had the effect of closing two weekly stock sales at which an Averac,e of 60 head of cattle and over 100 sheep are disposed of. * *

* - Mr. E. G. Hemmerde, M.P., speaking at a Liberal garden party at Highgate, on Satur- day night, said the Tory party were rather disturbed about the Liberal land proposals. They had got hold of the information that there was a committee at work on these questions, of which Mr. Lloyd George was the inspiring spirit, and of which he (Mr. Hemmerde) was one of the members. Their opponents had a horrible suspicion that the policy which upset their hopes in North- West Norfolk might upset their hopes still more lamentably at the next General Elec- tion; and he thought it would. He did not fight North-West Norfolk without knowing that powerful forces were ready to take up the taxation of land values, and he predicted that this was the question upon vhich the next General Election would be fought. e, *

At Sotheby's, which may be likened to a little eddy to which the most curious documents are in the habit of finding their way, a Cromwellian letter has turned up delightfully full ofOld Noll's shrewd common- sense and peculiarities. It was 'written at the end of January, 1643, when he was raising a body of cavalry to resist the advance of Lord Capel. The directions about "what captaines of horse you chuse, what men bee mounted" show the soldier as well as the man of the world. " A few honest men are better than numbers" is one of his maxims. Another is "If you bee able to foyle a force att the first cominge of itt, you will have reputation." But the best passage is his blunt "I had rather have a plaine russett coated captaine that knowes what bee fights for, and loves what hee knowes, than that wch you call a gentleman and is nothing else."—Country Life.

* * The Bill Ipromoted by the Bishop of Here- ford for the suppression of starting price betting and the many public incitements to gambling, is an exceedingly drastic measure, but not more so than is necessary in view of the serious evils it is designed to remedy. Already there are indications that its second reading in the House of Lords on the 11th inst. will be influentially opposed in the gambling interest, though even amongst betting men themselves there are those who frankly admit many legitimate causes for complaint in the existing order of things, especially as regards the wholesale distribu- tion of literature by turf tipsters and start- ing-price betting bookmakers. The penalties which the Bill seeks to impose upon offenders against any of its provisions are sufficiently heavy to induce any but the most case hardened to pause before taking the risk of incurring them.

* * *

In their candid or unguarded moments Tariff Reformers are constantly providing their own refutation of the fairy tales of Protectionist prosperity which they so dili- gently propagate from political platforms. When Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., sent his glowing message from Germany about the blissful lot of the dwellers in that Protec- tionist paradise he little imagined that one of the leading Tariff Reform authorities— Mr. Ellis Barker—would be so unkind as to write an article in the "Nineteenth Century " setting forth a few plain truths with regard to Ger Man trade stagnation and and declaring with hrutal frankness that "Germany is politically, militantly, economically, administratively, and morally on the down grade." Mr. Barker's words were quoted with telling effect by Mr. Samuel Gleave in the course of his excellent speech in defence of Free Trade at the open- air meeting on Monday evening. * *

•Discussing Mr. Lloyd George's speech, the "Manchester Guardian" says: For the towns, the " freedom of the land" suggests a very different set of ideas. In one sense land is only too free, for it escapes contribution to the rates of the towns whose industry makes its value. But if there is one fact about local government that is beyond dispute, it is that its activities cannot be much further extended on the present basis of rating. The whole expense of local government falls entirely on industry, and not equit- ably as between different forms of industry. The shopkeeper is over-assessed as compared with a prosperous physician or barrister, because the nature of his business demands a greater floor-space. He often pays far more rates for premises in which he makes £3OO a year than another who occupies a single room in which he makes £3,000. But what- ever grievance one kind of industry may have as compared with another,all industries have an equal grievance against the land which contributes nothing, and whose value Itheir energy and enterprise are constantly sending up. For the cities, therefore, the freedom of the land means the abolition of the privilege under which the landowner actually taxes industry for his own benefit. Wealth in land must contribute to the cost of city government like other forms of wealth.

The public have heard a great deal lately from the source of the Press, concerning the dangers of the common house fly. The latest news is that Colne Corporation,acting on the advice of their medical officer, have declared war on the house-fly, and during the past few days, handbills have been distributed to all householderk in the borough, pointing out that health is seriously endangered by the winged insect, by reason of its disease- spreading propensities. In his report, the Medical Officer gives instruction on how best to get rid of the fly. The following are a few " don'ts " from the report: "Don't allow flies in your house " ; " Don 't permit them near your food " ; "Don't buy food-stuffs where flies are tolerated " ; "Don't eat where flies have access to food."

Singular Accident. Miss Jennie Rudd, of Warrington, was riding on the outside of a promenade tramcar near the North Pier, Blackpool, on Saturday, when the head of the trolley became entangled with the overhead equipment, broke, and fell on the lady's head, cutting through her hat and hair. A severe scalp wound was inflicted and had to be stitched.

BABY CARRIAGES, special value at 395. Cd. Go-carts from 10s. 6d.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt. The Church Lads' Brigade. Last week-end was spent under canvas by the members of the St. Paul's Company of the Church Lads' Brigade. After parading the town on Walking Day, the company assembled at its headquarters, and at 2.30 proceeded to Sutton Weaver, where Mr Walker had kindly placed a couple of fields at their disposal. The work of pitching the camp had been done by the cyclists, who had worked hard to com- plete the arrangements in a very short space of time. Reveille was sounaed at six o'clock on Saturday, and a little later the company were doing com- pany drill and the band- receiving their usual instruction. This lasted until eight o'clock when " cook-house " was sounded, followed by a route march in the vicinity of Aston. On Sunday morning a church parade was arranged, when 51 attended service at Frodsharn Parish Church. Captain Henshall was assisted in the command by Lieut. Henshall ; also included in the parade were Sergt.-Major Botomley, Sergt.-Instructors Ashworth (cyclists) and Gibson (ambulance). The bugle band in charge of Sergt.-Bugler Goodwin acquitted itself with credit. When not on duty the lads spent the time in Frodsham. The camp was struck on Sunday evening and the company were on the homeward march a little before seven o'clock, reaching Warring- ton about nine o'clock. Captain Henshall and , Sergt.-Major Bottomley addressed the lads and complimented them on their behaviour in camp.

St. Helens Cable Co.'s Trip. The annual staff and works trip of the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co., Ltd., took place on Saturday and about 250 took the opportunity of again visiting that ever favourite resort, Blackpool. Special arrange- ments had been made with the Tower and Winter Gardens and these great centres of attraction were taken advantage of during the morning as the weather was very threatening. Fortunately it cleared up after dinner and allowed everybody to see Blackpool at its best, and also to take advantage of the numerous attractions at South Shore. Although the return train was rather late, it nevertheless contained a very happy and contented (though tired) party. TENNIS, Croquet, Cricket and other outdoor gnmer.— J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt.

Colours Delayed at Warrington, An unfortunate hitch occurred in connection with the presentation to the Lord Bishop of Liverpool, of the old colours of the 4th King's (Liverpool) Regiment. The ceremony was arranged to take place at noon on Saturday in the Lady Chapel of the Liverpool' Cathedral. Lord Derby presented new c lours to the battalion on Thursday, and it was intended that the old colours should be conveyed by an escort of 50 men for presentation to the Lord Bishop, in order that they might be plased alongside the old colours of the Ist Battalion, sent from Ferrnoy, in the Lady Chapel. At noon the Lord Bishop was present to receive the colours, with Cancels Billbrough, Harford, Howell, Grenstead, Rev. W. J. Adams, and a number of other clergy to assist at the ceremony. The regimental baggage was being conveyed from Kendal, where the men had been undergoing training, to Warrington, and en route the luggage van was shunted into a siding, and was four hours late before it reached Orford Barracks. In the meantime a large congregation had assembled in the Lady Chapel to witness the ceremony, but owing to the delay the majority of them had to leave. The Lord Bishop had also an engagement in another part of the diocese, and was unable to remain to receive the colours, which did not arrive until about half-past three o'clock, the c dour party having had to wait at Warrington for their full-dress uniform, so that the presentation might be made with due ceremony. Canon Billbrough, as sub-dean of the Cathedral, received the colours, which were presented by the colonel commanding the regiment. A short service was held, during which the colours were laid on the altar, after which they were placed in position in the ambulatory of the Lady Chapel.

Via Ship Canal to New Brighton. The annual excursion of the employees of Messrs. William Robertson, Ltd., Latchford, took place on Saturday last. The outing this time partook of a novel character. One of the magnificent tenders of the Alexander Towing Co., Liverpool, the S.S. " Herald " was chartered to convey the party from Latchford Locks to New Brighton. A start was made at 650 with about 300 passengers. A further con- tingent from the Appleton Works was embarked at Runcorn. New Brighton was reached after a very enjoyable sail at 10.13. Immediately upon landing all proceeded to New Brighton Tower Grounds where an enjoyable luncheon was provided in the Algerian Cafe. After luncheon, Mr. F. W. Wareing, manager of the Company, read a telegram which he had received from Mr. and Mrs. Robertson expressing heartiest wishes for a very enjoyable day. Mr. Wareing was sorry that Mr. and Mrs. Robertson were unable to be present, but they were with them in spirit. He thought that such a gathering as this should not disperse without some tribute being paid to the great amount of work which had been willingly put into the arrangements of the function by the secretary of the company, Mr. T. Collins, and also Mr. Humphreys. Be also thought that he should express every thanks to Mr. Humberset and his staff for the very efficient manner in which he had provided the luncheon. Mr. N. Cook also made some complimentary remarks as to management of the function. Mr. T. Collins responded suitably on behalf of himself and Mr. flumphreys. The afternoon was spent in and about the grounds, full advantage being taken of the various amusements provided. At 6.30 the party crossed by ferry boat to Liverpool where they embarked again on the S.S. " Herald." The return journey was commenced at 7 35 p.m and Latchford Locks reached at 10 30 p.m. The journeys by boat were considerably enlivened by the efforts of a small orchestra which had been arranged, and each one of the party thoroughly enjoyed themselves. All agreed that ,it was the most interesting and enjoyable trip which so far had been held in connection with this firm.

TOWN COUNCIL. SALARIES INCREASED. - - ALLEGED POLICE BOYCOTT.

The monthly meeting of the Town Council will he held on Tuesday evening, at six o'clock, when the following among other business will be considered :

INCREASE OF OFFICIALS' SALARIES. The following increases of salaries •are recommended for approval. Mr. M. Nichol, chief constable, from £4OO to £450. Appointed 1037 at £330.. Mr. A. T. fiallaway, deputy town clerk, from £3OO to £350; Appointed in 1905 at £175, rising to £250. ALLEGED BOYCOTTING OF POLICE. The Water Committee enquired into the alleged boy- cotting in. the Force, and are thoroughly satisfied that there is no ground whatever for such allegation. The offer of the Committee of increased pay with a modified pension scale was taken advantage of by the whole of the members of the Force with the exception of ten men with 20 years' service and over, and three with 12, 7, and 5 years respectively. No application of any description has been received from the men with over 20 years' service. With regard to the remaining three, one applied, in writing, to be allowed to come under the new scale, but the application was not recom- mended to the Committee owing to the irregular manner- in which the Constable acted after having applied. With ' regard, to the second Of the three. the Constable con- cerned applied verbally respecting the increased pay, and was informed that he was then too late to come under the new conditions, but that if he sent in an application it wovld receive consideration by the Watch Committee, but up to the present no written application has been made. The third officer has since accepted the new scale and would have done so before had it not been for the fact of his imagining that he would lose his merit badge.

The Committee deplore that Members of the Council should make unfounded charges against the discipline of the Force, which are calculated to create distrust in the mind of the public, and mischief and dissatisfaction among the members of the Force.

CORPORATIONS AND INCOME TAX.

At a meeting of the Finance and Estate Committee, a letter was read from the Association of Municipal Cor- porations with respect to Income Tax charged on Corpora- tions and stating that the case of the Leeds Corporation v. Sugden had been decided in favour of the Corpora- tion by the Court of Appeal, and that the Crows had given notice of appeal to the House of Lords. In the event of the decision being affirmed by. the House of Lords no further money would be required, but should the decision be in favour of the Crown, the sum of about £3,600 would be required to cover the costs, and the Association ask the Corporation in the latter event to contribute in proportion to the rateable value of all Boroughs concerned. Resolved. That in view of the great importance to municipalities of the question involved, and in the event of an , adverse decision of the House of Lords, this Committee agree to contribute towards the cost of the action in proportion to the rateable value of all Cor- porations concerned. WINWICK ROAD BOWLING GREEN.

The Town Clerk laid before the Finance Committee the sanction of the Local Government Board to the borrow- ing of the sum of £765, being the estimated cost of constructing a Bowling Green on land in Winwick-road, and also their consent to the appropriation of such land for that purpose. DEATH OF MR. W. H. BLECKLY J.P.

Warrington has lost one of its most highly respected commercial leaeers ny the death of Mr. William Henry Bleckly, J.P , Thelwall Lea, Thelwall, deputy- chairman of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company Limited, who passed away at the Pwllycrochan Hotel, Colwyn Bay at half-past ten on Tuesday morning. For some time Mr. Bleckly's health bad been far from satisfactory and he had been staying at Colwyn Bay for the past five or six weeks in the hope of recuperating. The news of his death was received with deep regret in Warrington, for Mr. Bleckly always took a very keen and active interest in the industrial development of the town, with which the welfare of the workers is so closely bound up. Although he had reached the age of 72, Mr. Bleckly led a remarkably busy life, and, until his recent breakdown in health, was most punctual in his daily attendance at the offices of the great firm whose pros- perity he had done so much to promote. He was one of heads of the iron trade and was honoured throughout the commercial world for his many sterling qualities. As soon as the intimatian of Mr. Bleckly's death reached Warringt,in, fligs were displayed at half-mast at the works ot Messrs. Pearson aud Knowles and Rylands Bros. Ltd. (of which the deceased gentleman was also a director) and at the Town Hall. Mr. Weekly, who was a native of Northaller ton, Yorkshire, was educatEd at Queenwood College, Hants. Robert Owen, the Socialist, founded the institution as a home for Communism, but as the experiment was not successful, it was, in the year 1817, converted into a school which was destined to be famous by its association with such celebrities as Henry Fawcett, Professors Tyndal, Hirst and Debug. Mr. Blecklv was the eldest son of the late Mr. Henry Bleckly, J.P., who many years ago was one of Warrington's most prominent public men, occupying the Mayoralty in 1866-7-8 and also acting for a period as chairman of the Warring- ton Board of Guardians. He was also elected chairman of the Quarter Sessions at Liverpool, on the retirement of the late Lord Derby. In many respects the late Mr. Henry Bleckly and his two sons may be

LA.IE Mt. W. H. BLECKLY.

said to have been the pioneers of the iron trade in Lancashire. Mr. W. H. Sleekly joined his fatner at Dallam Forge in 1856, when the iron trade in Lancashire, to quote from "Lancashire Leaders," was "in its infancy." Staffordshire, which used to be the . . . . . . .

great centre Of the British iron trade, and indeed, of the iron trade of the world, looked down on the newly- developing trade in Lancashire as an experiment not likely to survive the test of competition. The trade, however, fl wished remarkably, and Hallam Forge was followed by Bevvsey Forge, in which Mr. Bleckly's family were again the ' prime movers. Later on the two concerns were amalgamated, the collieries of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles acquired, and the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Limited, was founded. The iron and steel department of this great c‘meern, which produces an enormous quantity of finished iron and sted per week, has been managed for a long time by Mr. W. H. Bleckly and his brother Mr. J. J. Bleckly, the inventor of the celebrated and world-renowned repeating wire-rolling mill. Upwards of 2,000 hands are employed, altogether apart from the colliery department. Mr. Bleckly was a vice-president and hon. treasurer of the Iron and Steel Institute and a trustee of the Bessemer Medal Fund, having been appointed in succession to the late Sir Lo • ihian Bell. He was placed on the Commission of the Peace for the County of Lancaster, in Jubilee year, 1887 and his attendance (la the bench at the Warrrington County Petty Sessions was as frequent as the demands of business life would allow. In politics. Mr. Bleckly was a staunch Conservative and Tariff Reformer, but did not often address public meetings. He possessed cultured literary tastes and his extensive library contained many rare and valuable works. Mr. Bleckly married the youngest daughter of Mr. John Johnson, of Easingwold, near York, who survives him. The family consists of three sons and one daughter.

MACUSTERIA L REFERENCES.

At the County Sessions on Wednesday' the Chair- man (Mr. S. R. Armitage) said they had met that morning under sad conditions owing to the death of Mr. W. H. Weekly. who had been a magistrate at that court since 1887. They deeply bemoaned hie death. In the early part of his connection with that court he was always a regular attender, but in later years, owing to increased pressure in hia business life, he bad not been able to give full attention to the court. He was one of the heads of one of the largest concerns in the town, employing a great amount of ' labour. The magistrates desired to extend their deep sympathy with the deceased gentleman's relatives. Mr. H. Greenall (magistrates clerk), said that as one of the officials of the court he would like to associate himself with the remarks of the Chairman. Mr. Browne, on behalf of the solicitors practising at the court, said that many years ago he was in a similar position to the one he occupied that morning, in having to pay a tribute to Mr. Bleckly's father. Many years had gone by since that day and much water had passed under the bridges. He had received a beautiful letter from the deceased gentlemen in reply to remarks he had made about his father. He had always found him a most just and generous magistrate at that court. THE FUNERAL

Impressive scenes were witnessed at the funeral which took place at Thelwall Church yesterday after- noon. The respect in which the deceased gentleman was held was evidenced by the large and representa- tive gathering awaiting the arrival at Bank Quay station of the 2 26 train on which the body bad been conveyed from Colwyn Bay. A special train left Warrington for Thelwall at 2,50 and many who were anxious to pay a last tribute of esteem availed them- selves of this means of reaching the village. Mean- while others had set out to walk the distance to Thelwall and hundreds thronged the Knutsford road along which the cortege proceeded. First came three landaus filled with beautiful wreaths, then the hearse followed by thirty seven coaches, the rear being brought up by a hurry on which were borne the exquisite floral tributes from the employes of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. Ltd. At Latchford Swing Bridge about 500 workmen of the firm joined the cortege and headed it to the church. lAs the accommodation within the building was very limited, only intimate friends and a few prominent representatives were able to obtain admission. The Vic ar(Rev T Pym Williamson) conducted a brief but impressive service. The choir, consisting of twenty male voices, sang Psalm XC to a chant by Barnby and the hymns "Now the labourer's task is o'er,' and 1" On the resurrection morning." Mr. W B Cook was at the organ and played the Dead March in Saul, Beethoven's " Marche Funebre," " 0 Rest in the Lord" and "I know that my Redeemer livetb." The coffin, which was of polished English oak, with inside metallic shell and brass mountings, was inscribed: " William Henry meekly. died July 2nd, 1912 aged 72 years." The Vicar officiated at the graveside, and on the conclusion of the rites the public were admitted to the church yard to view the profusion of floral tributes which had been placed over the grave. The funeral arrangements were carried out by Messrs. Kendal Milne, and Co., Manchester.

INSURANCE ACT AND THE INFIRMARY.

THE EFFECT ON WORKINGMEN'S CONTRIBUTIONS.

The possible effect of the National Insurance Act on the contributions of the workingmen to the Warrington Infirmary was discussed at a special meeting of the Workingmen's Committee held at the Dispensary on Thursday evening. Alderman T Burton (chairman of the committee) presided over 3 good attendance of representatives of various worke. The CHAIRMAN said he was not now connected with any works in the town and he did not know what the feeling at the different works was in regard to the effect of the Act. He did know, however, that at many of the leading works they had sick clubs and when the contributions to those clubs were collected the contributions to the Infirmary had been collected at the same time. He took it that, when the Act came into operation, many of the clubs would be dispensed with, but whether that would effect the collections for the institution he did not know. If it did, it would be a very serious matter for the Infirm- ary. At some of the works the contributions had been collected through the office, but now that the sick clubs would in all probability be disbanded, would the office staffs still go to the trouble of collecting any small contributions that the workmen might feel disposed to give to the Infirmary? Mr. HAMPSON, secretary of the Infirmary, said that in making the half-yearly collection from the works he had been led to anticipate a likelihood of contribu- tions falling off, and consequently that meeting was called. Only 25 per .cent of the patients treated at the Infirmary would be insured persons, leaving 75 per cent, to be attended to on the present lines. That percentage was composed practically of women and children. It was a serious matter, for if the contri- butions fell off who would look after them ? In case of accidents the patients would want to be brought to the Infirmary for treatment. If they relied upon a private doctor, be might not be in his surgery just when an accident case was brought in. Last year the amount collected from the works was 5.1,245, as against .A:1,122 in the preceding year. The total amount raised for the Infirmary from all sources was C 4,500. In the course of the discussion, it was stated that the sick club at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles would bra continued to the end of the year and presumably the contributions to the Infirmary would also be kept up. The CHAIRMAN said he had had an intimation that at one works in the town the usual contribution to the Infirmary was not deducted from the wages last week. That was the only case he had heard of. Mr. StrAw said that if all works followed the plan of a periodical deduction from wages on behalf of the Infirmary, there would be no danger of a falling off. For the life of him he could not see why the Act should be any menace to the institution. There were hundreds of men who contributed to the Infirmary who were not touched by the employment section of the Act and there were many hundreds who would benefit by part I. of the measure. A scheme was to be put into operation at Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.'s which would benefit almost every man about the works.

One delegate expressed the opinion that men earn- ing IBs. a week would not be likely to continue their contributions to the Infirmary when they had to pay Id. per week fa- insurance. On the other hand another representative was equally emphatic that the workmen would not think of refusing to help the institution.

The CHAIRMAN appealed to the delegates, in any cases where there might be some doubt as to future procedure, to use all their influence to induce the workmen to keep up their contributions. Ile also assured the meeting that the Insurance Act was not likely to interfere with the medical treatment of the patients at the Infirmary. Mr E HORROCKS said he sincerely hoped the contributions would not fall off but the natural tendency was for a workman to say that if he bad to contribute to a State fund for sickness he would not • give to the same extent to the Infirmary. Seeing that the usefulness of the institution was already crippled for want of contributions and might be still further hampered in the future, there was all the greater need for a committee like that, which represented the working classes of the town, to speak with unmistakable voice about the municipalization of hospitals. The time was absolutely ripe for such a movement. If they did not municipalise the institutions, there should at least be a National grant. Mr DAINTITH said be understood that in case of an accident a private doctor would not have to give an insured person attention. The patient would there- fore have to be sent to an Infirmary and the need for contributions would be as great as ever. Mr. A. C. BINKS pointed out that the majority of those who contributed to the Infirmary were workmen who were outside the wage limit for introduction to the Infirmary, and who were merely seeking to help those in more needy circumstances. He believed that if a vote were taken throughout the works at the present moment, there was not a man in the whole of the works who would not subscribe to the upkeep of the Infirmary, if only to keep it open in case of acci- dents. It was not a question of sickness. The medical fraternity would deal with sickness under the Act, but whatever happened under the national scheme, it could not take from a centre like Warring- ton an institution that would deal with minor and severe accidents immediately. It was a necessity, and therefore he believed the people of Warrington were too judicious to withdraw their subscriptions from such an institution at this juncture. The general feeling of the meeting, as finally summed up by the Chairman, was that the workmen would continue their subscriptions as usual— at all events, to the end of the year. The Chairman characterised the result of the gathering as most encouraging.

Warrington Captain in Motor Smash. Captain Winkler, of Orford Barracks, had a remark- able escape from injury in a motor collision, which happened at the White Barn Hotel corner, Cuddington, near Northwich, on Monday afternoon. The captain was proceeding in a car to Tarporley, and Dr O'Neill, of Altrincham, was returning home in a car from Cuddington, after visiting an invalid, when the collision occurred. The doctor's car was the more ' seriously damaged and was swung right round, whilst he was thrown violently into the road, being badly bruised and shaken. The Captain retained his seat and escaped injury, and, singular to relate, a small pet dog, which sat beside him, aiso remained seated and apparently unconcerned after the impact. Both gentlemen went to the residence of Mr. G. N. Thomp- son, hunting stable proprietor, which is close by, and Captain Winser afterwards proceeded to Tarporley in one of Mr. Thompson's traps. The doctor also returned home later in the evening.

Borough Police Court. SATURDAY.—Before Messrs J W Hayward(in the chair A J Jolley, It T Fairclough and Dr Fox. OLD LADY INJURED.—EmiIy Brown, Mersey-street, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly early that morning and also with unlawfully wounding M ry Burke.—The Chief Constable said that at about 12.35 that morning the prosecutrix, who was 78 years of age, was on her doorstep when the prisoner cane up. The; had a few words and the prisoner vent to the side- board and picked up a pot with which she struck the prosecutrix on the face causing a nasty wound. The prosecutrix had to be taken to the 'Min-nary for treat- ment. Be asked for a remand for a week.—The magis- trates granted the application.

TUKSDAY.—Before Councillors H Roberts (in the chair) and C Jackson Holmes and -Messrs J Hepherd and T Locker.

ASSAULT CHARGE DISMISSED —Daniel Ilighsm, Fox-street, was charged with having assaulted Lucy Lee, of 113, Kimberley-street, on Saturday. The .com- plainant said she was returning home down WeMeld- street in the afternoon when the prisoner came across the road and struck her in the face, knocking her against the railings and rendering her insensible for some time. She asked the bench to deal leniently with the prisoner as he had some children dependent on him.—Prisoner alleged that both the complainant and her husband were drank and that the complainant started cursing him. He pushed her to make her go home. The case was dismissed.

ASSAULT.—AIice Ingham, Fox-street, was charged with having assaulted Margaret Holroyd of Kimberly- street. Complainsnt said the prisoner threw her down In the street ,nd in falling her arm was severely injured.—Higham was bound over for six months and ordered to pay the costs of the case. WEDNESDAY—Before Mr J W Hayward (in the chsir), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor C Jackson Holmes and Mr WT Williams.

ALLEGED WIFE DESE-RTION.--John Caddy, A, Elaine- street, was summoned by his wife Emily, of 7, Foster- street, for desertion. Mr. Jenkins, who appeared for the defendant, said he desired to prevent the domestic troubles being discussed in Court and suggested that the defendant should agree to a separation and allow his wife 10s. per week, the wife to have the custody of the two children.—Mr. Steel, on behalf of the com- plainant, accepted the offer and the magistrates made the order as suggest4d by Mr. Jenkins. ASSAULTED HIS SlSTER.—William Lyons, 26, Earle- street, was charged with having been drunk and dis- orderly and also with having assaulted his sister, Annie Bradshaw. The sister said her brother had hit her in the eye, severely injuring the eye-ball, whilst under the influence of drink. Lyons was sent to prison for one month.

TIIERSDAY.—Before Mr J Rymer Young (in the chair) Messrs.) Upson, A J Jolley snd Dr Naden. BREAKING WINDOWS His MANlA.—Peter Corraboy, 15, Ship-yard, was charged with wilfully and unlawfully breaking a plate glass window at the establishmnnt of Messrs Eustance and Co., Jewellers, Sankey-street, on Wednesday, doing damage to the extent of I6.—The Chief Constable stated that at about 10.30 the previous morning ex-Sergeant Evans was in Sankey-street when he saw the prisoner go deliberately up to the window and push his foot through twice, doing considerable damage to the window and the stock, The magistrates could find that a prima facie case had been proved so that the prisoner could be committed for trial. He asked that the case should be adjourned until Monday. The prisoner bad been previously charged with a similar offence.—The Chairman : Is it another demonstration of the suffragist movement ?—The Chief Constable : I don't think he has enough sense for it. It seems to be a mania of his, and the real solution for it is prussic acid.

FRIDAY.— Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Pierpoint, James Evans, Smethurst, Messrs G Pilling, T Locker, S Wright and A J Jolley. WIFE DESERTION.—John Bennett, 95, 17411fleld-street, was summoned by his wife Catherine of 42, Garibaldi- street for desertion and the wife asked for an order.— Complainant stated that her husband had deserted her three weeks ago. He then gave her 15s. and had not given her much since. His sister had parted them and she was the root of all the trouble. The defendant had said he would not go hack to her again. There were three children aged 2 6 and 8 respectively, and she, complainant, had been in the Workhouse.—lnsoector Wainwright said he had known the family to have been at loggerheads for two years. The house was nearly devoid of furniture. The defend3nt had told him that ,he would not go back to his wife and also that she could do as she liked. Both parties were faulty. The man drank and the woman did not attend to her house- hold duties as she ought to do.—The magistrates granted the wife an order for 12s. per week, she to have the custody of the children.

County. Petty Sessions. WEDNESDAY—Before Mr S R Armitage (in the chair), Mesars C E Parker and linger C Parr. ALLEGED UNJUST WEIGHTS.—Isaac Bell, Warburton Bridge, was summoned for having in his possession for the use of trading, on the 14th June, at Rixton, an unjust 71b weight and a loz. and one 40z. weights which were unstamped.—Superintendent Shepherd said that the weights were found on a greengrocer's cart which bore defendant's name. The defendant's son was in charge of the cart.—Mr. Browne, for the defendant said that the greengrocery business was entirely managed by the defendant's wife, Bell himself having nothing to do with it. Ihe defendant bad a 71b. weight which he used for rivetting and not for trading purposes. The boy had lost a 71b. weight and took the one used for rivetting. The father had not missed it. Other weights had been bought by the wife without defendant troubling about the matter, as he had nothing to do with the business. The case was dismissed, the magistrates advising that the wife's name should be painted on the cart instead of the husbands. The superintendent applied for a summons against the wife. Mr Browne offered to pay the costs of the case but this the superintendent would not accept. The summons arts granted.
