ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

-- One of the most attractive repertories seen at the Rylands-street house for a considerable time has been presented this week by Ethel Van Praagh and J. Edward Whitty's company. The plays selected lend themselves admirably to twice nightly production and so much that is of compelling interest is packed within the compass of an hour and threequarters' entertain- ment, that the audiences have been held enthralled. The company is a most capable one, the members readily adapting themselves to the widely con- trasting parts which they have been called upon to assume and each performance has been characterised by briskness and finish. On Monday and ITuesday evenings a clever French play, "The eyes of the world," was the attraction. It was a somewhat sordid story of intrigue, relieved by a mother's passionate devotion to her daughter, and some of the powerful incidents worked up excitement to a high pitch. Miss Ethel Van Praagh, who is an emotional actress of exceptional talent, achieved a notable success as the much-wronged and long- suffering Helene de L'Orme. It was a trying part but she rose to the occasion and invested every scene with intense realism. Mr. Sydney Bland gave a remarkably fine portrayal of the despicabls and shameless Hector de L'Orme, his acting being convincing in its freedom and natural- ness. Miss Dorothy Devi hurst was a charming Avice and Mr. R. S. Pittard as Octave de Carnac was the personification of the impulsive lover. Mr Guy P Ellis gave a spirited presentment of Gaston de Carnac snd Miss Flora Hastings showed marked talent in the thankless role of Remo de Carnac. Mr. J Edward Whitty and Miss May Neilson provided a humorous element as the bliefless barrister and his actress wife.

On Wednesday large audiences followed with delight a highly creditable production of the old favourite "Lady of Lyons." Mr Sydney Bland must be complimented upon an artistic triumph as Claude Melnotte, the peasant masquerading as Prince, his characterization being so powerful as to grip the " house " from beginning to end. Miss Ethel Van Praagh added to her laurels as Pauline, and all the other parts were in capable hands. "David Garrick " was played on Thursday, chief honours going to Mr. Sydney Bland, who was really brilliant in the title part. Last evening "Facing the World " was performed, and it is billed for this evening.

FOUN D. The secret of happiness. It is health. So bear in mind that Steedman's Powders correct the slight stomach troubles whiek make children so fretful, and their parents depressed.

A Bishop and the Insurance Act.

The Bishop of Birmingham, writing in his "Diocesan Magazine" on the Insurance Act, urges the clergy to do all they can to help their people to work the Act. "We may feel that the matter has been unduly hurried," he says, "there are points which we should like to see amended, but these facts will not excuse the folly of being unready to make Use of the provisions of the Act. would recommend those who are still ignorant to write to the Commissioners at Wellington House, Buckingham Gate, London, for literature for general information. It is also a good thing to have a parish meeting, with a good speaker, to explain the Act, and then, before the expiration of the three months from July 15th, I hope our people will be members of some approved society, and so in full receipt of the benefits of the Act."

===== 0002601_19120706_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
" The Best Sunday School.

The Parish Church Magazine says :—" The following letter has been sent us by the Rev. If. B. Firth :—St. James' Vicarage, Warrington, May 21st, 1912. My dear Rector,—l have been reading an old volume of the "Quarterly Review," dated 1846, and have come across an article entitled " Education of the people." There is an extract which I think will interest you, and which lam copying out for you. It runs thus—We read this sentence in Mr. Watkins' report with peculiar satisfac- tion : "In the best Sunday school which I have visited —best, I mean, as to influence and permanent effects— that of the Parish Church at Warrington, the school hours are not more than one and a quarter in the morn- ing, and three-quarters of an hour in the afternoon."

Rev. E. C. E. Carleton on Ministers' " Lack of Honesty." "As we go to press," says Holy Trinity Parish I Magazine," a remarkable debate in the House of Lords is reported, which we would recommend our readers to peruse very carefully. The Vicar has often reflected (ungenerously, perhaps some may think) on the lack of accuracy and candour and even honesty on the part of the Ministers conducting the attack upon the ‘Velsh Church ; tut nothing he has ever said can equal the condemnation that the bare report of this debate inflicts upon Mr. McKenna. It is not that all his statistics regarding the work of the Church and of Noncon- formity in the town of Cardiff are wrong, and culpably wrong, for he had the means of verifying his state- ments in his own office ; but that when his attention was drawn to the errors made by him, no satisfactory explanation was forthcoming nor any amends made for statements that could only be set right by an apology, the reult being that the Minister was subjected to a reproof from the Speaker of the House of Commons not often heard. The Bishop of St. Asaph deserves the thanks of all Churchmen for calling attention to the matter in the House of Lords."

===== 0002601_19120706_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's Presbyterian Church.

The attitude of Christ towards the Bible of His day was the theme of the Rev. W. Reid's address on Sunday evening. Without doubt Christ reverenced the scriptures. He directed His opponents to its search, and the young Ruler to the observance of its law. Like the old prophet He made His appeal to it, and claimed for it a worthy obedience. At the same time He hesitated not to criticize it. It was not perfect and He made no attempt to bide its defects. He claimed for it a perfect origin, but an imperfaot channel, It was negative mainly, and preferred to look upon the works of a man rather than upon the motive which prompted those actions. But His was not an idle, meaningless criticism; He was prepared to complete its incompleteness, to fulfil the possibilities it contained, to call forth the bud into fuller blossom, and the blossom into perfect fruit. The old life, said the preacher is not capable of this until it has seen Christ. He hates law. To see Christ is to become His devoted slave, and to find that the old offending rules have become transformed into a spiritual code of love.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe Congregational Church.

The pulpit at this chu—rch— on Sunday was occupied, morning and evening, by the Rev. George Slack, of Wakefield. At the morning service in an address to young people he greatly interested them by his stories illustrative of the effect on their lives and work of the Spirit of the Master. His sermon was based on the challenge of Elijah to the priests of Baal on Mount Carmel and to the vindication of the true God. He proceeded to draw a comparison between the results obtained from heathenism, Buddhism, Mohammedanism, Confucianism and Christianity, and said whilst it would be entirely wrong to say that there was nothing noble and good in the old religions, yet it was indisputable that Christianity had done more than anything else to uplift humanity. Reference was made to " The Trampled Cross " by Joseph Hocking, and Harold Begbie's " Broken , Earthenware "as bearing evidence of the saving power of the gospel of Jesus Christ. In the evening the preacher based his discourse on the words " Underneath are the Everlasting Arms." He showed that although scientists and psychologists had revealed many things there were causes they could not fathom. Amid an ever-changing world there was an Everlasting God who had a great purpose and plan and whose arms were ever ready to strengthen and uphold. The Rev. A. J. Viner, F.T.S. (secretary of the Lancashire Congregational Union) will preach to-morrow (Sunday) and on the following Sunday (July 14th), the Rev. J. D. Jones, M.A., 8.D., of Bournemouth, will occupy the pulpit.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF MR. W. S. MIAREN, M.P.

Mr. Walter Stowe Bright M'Laren, MP. for the CI ewe Division of Cheshire, died on Sunday. Some time ago, the hon. member had a collapse, completely breaking down in health. He afterwards left London for Kent, intending to go to Switzerland, but although he rallied somewhat, he did not improve in health, and the end came not unexpectedly. The body was cremated on Thursday. The death of Mr. APLaren has caused a profound sensation at Crewe. Mr. M'Laren's death creates a vacancy in the Crewe division. He was the youngest son of the late Duncan M'Laren, a well-known citizen of Edinburgh. He was the brother of the late Lord M`Laren, of the Court of Session, and of Sir C. B. IV Laren, now Lord Abercon- way, and was born in 1853, his mother being a sister of John Bright. Educated at Craigrnount School, 'Edinburgh, and Edinburgh University, he married in 1883 Eva, the youngest daughter of the late William Muller, of Hilleide,lierte. He was a director of the great iron firm of Bolckovr, Vaughan, and Co., of Middlesbrough, and wrote a technical work on worsted and woollen spinning. He sat for the Crewe Division from 1886 until 1895, and was returned again in a by-election on April 30th, 1910. His majority at the last election was 1,701. The death of Mr. M`Laren will be keenly felt by the advocates of the Parlia- mentary enfranchisement of women, who were closely identified with him in this matter in the House of Commons.

LANDLORD AND TENANT.

RESULT OF THE SANKEY ACTION FOR ALLEGED ASSAULT.

Judgment for the Defendant.

His Honour Judge Stanger, K.C., delivered jt-dgment at the Warrington County Court on Thursday in the action brought by Arthurina Lowe, wife of Frank R Lowe, Mount Pleasant, Great Sankey, against Charles H Johnstone builder, Great Sankey, for £3O damages for an assault alleged to have been committed by the defendant on February 15th. The case for the plaintiff had been conducted by Mr A Browne and the defendant was represented by Mr. Burgis, barrister (instructed by Mr. T. E. Knowles)._

His Honour said— the case had lasted a considerable time and there was a conflict of evidence with regard to the main point. It was a matter which would perhaps involve somewhat serious consequences to the losing party and he had therefore taken the opportunity of refreshing his memory as to the evidence given at the earlier hearing in order that he might fully consider all the arguments on both sides. Although on the main point there was a conflict of evidence, yet, sith regard to a great many details, there was practically agreement between the parties as to what occurred. There was no doubt that the plaintiff and her husband were about to leave the house of which the defendant was the landlord and they were moving to a house not very far off. On the date of the alleged assault the furniture was being removed but the workmen were not actually in the house at the time of the events which formed the subject of the action. Ft was also not in dispute that the thfendant sent a foreman joiner and two other workmen to go through the house in order to report to him on the condition of the premises. Two of the men interviewed the plaintiff in her garden and,although she was at flrst unwilling that they should go into the house, she afterwards gave them permission. Later a third man was allowed to go through. After they bed been in the house about half an hour the defendant himself came on the scene.lt was not in doubt at all that the plaintiff was unwilling that he should go through the house and it was also clear that some- thing which might be descrihed as a struggle took

THE STATE BARGE. This week, for the first time, a King and the Royal bargeman, Mr. W. G. East. have, Queen of England will be present at Henley been rehearsing with the State barge: Out Regatta. place on the staircase. But there was the point on which the parties differed and he had to decide who had given the correct account. Plaintiff stated that she was on the second or third step of the stairs and endeavouring to prevent the defendant going upstairs, and that be pushed her down, took her by the arm, and treated her in a manner which even if he nad a right to come into the house, would certainly amount to an assault. Defendant's story was that he was going upstairs and had reached the second or third step when the plaintiff threw her- self forward and, in an endeavour to prevent him going up, grabbed him by the legs. There- fore, it was contended, whatever injuries she 1 sustained she had brought upon herself. An argument which one always had to consider when there was a conflict of evidence was, whether on one side or the other, there was a possibility that there had been a mistake and that inaccurate evidence had been given not because the witness desired to say what was untrue and was prepared to commit perjury, but because,eitber 1 through defective recollection or observation or from 1 the excitement of the moment, his or her account was not entirely accurate, although given in good faith. Of course, one's experience in courts of justice was such that 000 could not rule out as impossible the theory that one side or the other had committed wilful perjury in a particular case. One would not, however, adopt that theory in the case of a respect- able person, against whose character nothing could be alleged, unless one was driven to it. Looking at the present case and considering the feelings which evidently were animating the plaintiff, he could not help thinking that if there was a mistake, it appeared to him much more likely that the plaintiff, rather than the defendant and his witnesses, was mistaken. Defendant might perhaps have been somewhat excited, but when one remembered that Mrs. Lowe sent for a policeman before the alleged assault took place and that, accord- ing to some witnesses, S3O was abusing the defendant I before the struggle, she certainly was in a very excited state. Moreover, there was no particular reason why the joiner, who was called in corrobora- I tion of the defendant, should not have given a truthful account if he was minded to do so. The next test one had to apply, although it was not conclusive, was whether one story was themore probable of the two It was argued very strongly by Mr. Burgis that the plaintiff's story was improbable. On the other hand it might be contended that if a person was telling an improbable story, it was to some extent a recommenda- tion, because if the witness intended to tell a lie he or she would not be so likely to make up an improbable story. Reviewing tbe evidence, his Honour said that if plaintiff's story was accurate defendant pushed her, not with his hands but with his body, from the second step to the ninth. It was said by Mr. Bnrgis—not without foundation—that that was a very remarkable story. Perhaps the more remarkable and improbable it was, the more one might think it was true, because if a person was desirous of inventing a story, he or she would have invented something a little less unlikely. On the other hand, if she was in a state of great excite- ment and endeavoured to prevent the defendant going upstairs, she might have given that account or the affair because her mind was confused as to the precise details of what occurred. The-defendant's version was that he had got on to the first or second step before any attempt was made to stop him, and then the plaintiff threw herselflorward, as in a Rugby football match, and grabbed his feet. Defendant tried, as of course be was entitled to do if that really occurred, to get his feet free. He freed one foot and then another but the plaintiff, he said, grabbed him and that procedure continued up several stairs. Defendant's case was that be was not guilty of an assault but that, if in trying to get free, some injury was done to the plaintiff it was not his fault but hers. He said it might be that in the struggle he might have caused the mark on her dress. Discussing the important question of corroboration, his Honour pointed out that there was no-one to corroborate plaintiff's story who could give any direct evidence of what took place. The only person who did see the incident was the foreman joiner who was called by the defendant and substantially corroborated the defendant's story. I Corroboration, however was not all confined to direct evidence. They might have it in indirect evidence. One 1 of the greatest difficulties that the plaintiff had to get over was this : When the plaintiff went to Mr Patten's house she showed him the mark on her dress, and that mark was as if the heel and sole of a boot had been pressed against it. She said that corroborated her story because it was more likely that it would be caused by the defendant trampling upon her than in the course of a struggle. He could not reject Mr Patten's evidence that there was that mark, and he had to consider how far such corroboration of the plaintiff's story had to outweigh the direct evidence of the defendant and his foreman joiner. There was also bleeding of the thumb. Ile (his Honour) did net attach much importance to that because plaintiff's own 1 statement was that she caught hold of the tail of defen- dants's coat and he pulled it from her, causing the injury. It was rather hard to accuse a man of assault because someone took hold of the tail of his coat and pulled it away. But the mark on the skirt was a different matter and he had had to consider it with very great care and anxiety. It was said there was further corroboration from Mr. Patten who however,did not see the allegedjassault. He gave some evidence which appeared tobe rather against the plaintiff and some which appeared to be in her favour. He (the Judge) accepted Mr. Patten as a witness who desired to say what was true but unfortunately be adopted a rather partisan attitude and answered questions in a manner which juetly caused something in the nature of a protest from Mr. Burgle. He resented perfectly proper questions as if Mr. Burgis were adopting an unduly

To MIM. Gaekell for to year's attend- ance on Sundays to August 22nd, 1790 2 0 0 The first Sunday teacher is William Bowman. Another is Mr Kirkpatrick who is paid for his services and the use of his school- room at the rate of £6 10s. per year, and who receives an additional £1 12s. 6d..per quarter for attendance on Sunday. His name disappears in 1791 and during the following year Mr Garner is paid at the rate of 2s. 6d. per week for Sunday duties. It would be interesting to know where the Sunday gatherings were held. No written chic is given to us but the list of subscribers leaves no doubt that the charity was attached to the Cairo-street Unitarian Church, and the attendance at worship arranged for in the constitution can hardly have referred to any other place. Payment for schoolroom rent to different individuals suggests that teaching was done according to convenience at various places. A.M. (To be continued.)

GOLF.

DEATH BEFORE DECORATION. _

_ James Green, colliery foreman, of Abram, near Wigan, who was awarded the Edward medal for con- spicuous bravery at the flooding disaster at Bamfurlong Colliery, has died before he could receive the decora- tion. He was seized with a serum illness after the announcement that the honour had been conferred on him, and died before he could make. the jou.ney Ito London to receive the medal at the King's hands.

NOTHING NEW UNDER THE SUN.

In the matter of the pay-as-you-enter tram cars, respecting which the Gateshead authorities+ have been indulgirg in a protonged "C ck-a-doodle-doo," the Warrin,;ton Corporation had a sound and indisputable claim to priority, as witness d by its introduction on the Longferd route several months ago. I have myself been familiar with something akin to it for considerably more than a score years. A quarter of a century ago, when I lived for a while in Southatiapt)n- street, off the Peckham-road, South London, there was a service of horse cars by which any of the principal budges cculd be reached for twopence. If, as occasionally happened, I vrinbcd to go by Vauxhall, there acre smaller cars taking that di colon from the neighboui hood of Camberwell-green, passing Kennington Park (close by which, in Manley- place I resided with my paws as a youngster, so that I know that locality well.) The little cars in question had no conductor. The driver, who bed sole charge, required payment of the fare as soon as the pas:eiger become comfortably seated, and, having behind.him an opening by which he c.ouldcommuuicate with the interior, he did not fail to log the memory of the absent-minded by a succession of sharp taps on the woodwork or by other significant features. For the accommodation of thoee needing change, he was furnished with sealed packets of coppers, and as the exact fare bad to be dropped into a box in full view, he did not handle any of the mcney but the silver coins which he was called upon to charge, and of course that was easily checked, as he was accountable for the silver equivalt nt for the copper with which he had parted on the j urney. THE BOY SCOUTS.

Here is a titbit which the nephews and nieces of cur friend who conducts the "Children's Corner" will appreciate : The little laddie s‘ho bad just joined the Boy Scouts bad gone to bed, and awoke from sleep, suddenly to remember that he bad not fulfided the rule that day. Be was in a sad frame of mind, and wondered what be could do to redeem his Scout vow. At last, a bright idea seized him. He jumped out of bed and gave his white nice to the cat I Seriously, however, and most earnestly, I do commend the move- ment in question, and I sincerely rejoice in the con- tinued progress and prosperity of the local Boy Scouts' Association. Without at present explaining in detail, let me just point out to those who are not already aware of it that before he b, c xnes a scout a boy takes the ccut's oath, thus : "On my honour I promise that I will do my best : 1, to do my duty t 3 God and my country ; 2, to help other rople at all times; 3, to obey the ke Alt law." lam satisfied that much good may b 3 co ,fi lently exp cod from this marvellously successful organi+ation ; and if anyone shculd for any leas )n challenge my position I will gladly give my reasons. For the moment I cannot conceive of the necessit:, MORRIS DANCING.

The little Morris dancers' delightful cmtribution to the pageantry on Walking Day reminded ma of the person in "Punch "who, so far from knowing any- thing of the dance itself, c rnfesaed that he "did not even know NS oMirt is was!" None of my readers will err so seriously, but many will doubtless be interested in the following compilation by a journalistic friend of my own : Did Milton, in " L'Allegro," refer to Morris dancing when he said : " While the merry bells ring round, And the jocund rebels sound? " It really seems not improbable, for bells are a distin- guishing feature cf the costume„nr t merely for ornament, but to be sounded. The bells were of unequal sizes, and differently named, as the fore bell, the second bell, the treble, the tenor or groat bell, and double belle. The principal dancer was more richly dressed than the rest. We read in an old play "He wants no cloths, for he bath a cloak laid on with gold lace, and an embroidered jerkin ; and thus he is marching hither like the fureman of a Morris dance." The number of dancers appears to have varied. The hobby horse was in later times inseparable from the Morris dance. It was a frame for the body, with a horse's head and tail, and covered with trappings down to the ground to cover the actcvr's feet. The ancient dress of the Morris dmcers approximated to the motley of the fool or jester, and there can be little doubt that the bladder on a whip was derived from the same court functionary, and was introduced to afford amusement. OUR "SUBMERGED TENTH."

_ In an editorial note on this subject in last issue, it was observed, as an experience of the Distress Committee operating here during the coal strike, that " sometimes the wife was in ignorance of what her husband really earned," In one County Court circuit in which I used to woi k, the Judge had a system ((Jr anything I know to the contrary, it may now be pretty general) whereby the earnings of the debtors of the labouring class werii ascertainable at a glance. The Court having the power to compel the attendance of a master or other respon- sible person to give the evidence, submitted in the alternative a form of certificate showing the average weekly wages of the defendant for the previous three months. Of course, those concerned readily took advantage of this, so that his Honour invariably had the information before him when he came to adjudicate. The defendant, being usually himself at work, would be represented by his wife - or, in the case of a young unmarried man, by his mother. Then would ensue some such passage as this between the Judge and and the woman : —" How much do you say he earns ?" " Well he hasn't brought me more tlian—(some cornparately small sum) for the last two or three months." "What becomes of the other 5s ? " "What other 53. ? " "The ss. that he does not give you ? " " I don't know about any 53. He gives me all for anything I know. That's what he tells me." "Well, I'll make an order for the payment of this debt by instalments of 15s. Far month, and you must tell him to pay the money out of what he has been in the habit of keeping back for himself withcut your knowledge ! " THE BAR !JAIN. The assault on a barmaid reported in this paper a week ago somehow brought to my mind how the lamented W. T. Stead championed the course of that class when it was proposed to make the calling illegal. In the "Review of Reviews" he wrote with sweet reasonableness. It is satisfactory to see that ministers have decided to turn a deaf ear to the earnest but mistaken section of their supporters who have been clamouring for the suppression of the barmaid. It may be right, expedient and necessary to suppress the public-house altogether, but until the State arrives at this conclusion, and acts upon it, there ought to be no resttiction of the equality of rights between men and women to earn their living behind the bar. Men have already monopolised cne kind of Bar. It is intolerable that they should usurp a similar monopoly at bars of another kind. To be a barmaid may not be the most ideal of all occupations for our daughters, but the same thing may be said of being a charwoman. For the matter of that, it is not equally an ideal occup-

tion for our sons to be potmen. he preseree of woman humanises, civilis‘s and moralires. Banish woman From the bar and you remove one of the best influences to be found in the public-house. If their hours are too long and the prods:on for their cooveniencl is itadequile, tho,e matters cm be seen to. But in the highest interests of hundreds of thousands of men %rho at present can call no woman by her Christian name except " the Maid cf the Inn," I am an uncompromis- ing opponent of the p-,tman monopoly, with which I am g'ad to see the Home Secretary will have nothing to do. William Crabtree, who was born in the neighbourhood, John Foster, the essayist—the man who had made Dr. Cliffo d what he was as a preacher and reformer Sutcliffe and Ward, were all eminent nun in tin ir denomination, who were born within a short distance of there. There is no part of England to which the Baptists are so indebted for pioneers as they are to the Iblifax and Hebden 13i idge district. So testified Dr Haslam, of Harrogate, at a recent stone-laying in connection with a new Baptist Chapel in those parts

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conductcd by UNCLE CALEB.

ABOUT ICEBERGS.

Since the great sea disaster of last April we have all been thinking about icebergs and I thought I would write a few lines about them to explain how they are formed. They are not, as most people think, frozen water in the strict sense of the word. Rather they are accumulations of snow which have formed first into glaciers and which then have broken off in the forms in which they are seen in the ocean. There are two types of glacier, coastal and valley. The coastal glacier is formed of snow which has gathered on the very edge of the rocky shores of the arctic and antarctic regions. It collects to an immense height and freezes solid. When the accumulation of snow is so increased that it projects beyond the rock edge of the land the glacier breaks off in huge, quadrangula masses or icebergs. The other kind of glacier is formed in the rocky valleys. The huge ice fields begin to slide and great pieces break off and fall into the sea. Of course the pieces or, as we must now call them, icebergs are of all sizes but commonly they float 90 or 100 feet above the water and have 700 or 800 feet below surface. Sometimes they are 600 to 700 square feet on the top and must weigh millions of tons. Lieutenant Peary measured one ice- berg and calculated that it weighed two thousand million tons.

These are the things that float southward and cause such anxiety and peril to seamen.

PLAIN CAT. Our neighbour's cat is Persian, the Jones's is Maltese ; Aunty's big Angora has feathers to her knees (At least they look like feathers), and a tail so big and white ; When that kitty meets a puppy dg, I tell you it's a sight. But when I ask, " What breed is mine, my pussy, sleek and fat ?" They laugh and pull my curls, and say, "I fear—just cat." It's true her eyes aren't yellow, and her tail is rather small ; I don't know if she ever had a pedigree at all (That big word means her mother, her grandma, too, they say ; That they all took prizes at a show, were marked a special way), What do I care for markings, for prizes, and all that ? My kitty's just as precious, if she is just cat !

She was the clearest kitten, all scamper Ind all fur ! Not one of all my pets could make me laugh like her. She may be very common, but 1 know she's good and

true, For she meets me when I come from schcd with a loving little mew ; And, when she's round, we never see a naughty mouse or rat, And I believe I love her better 'cause she's just plain cat !

MA

ANARCHY.

Of the various progressive movements established by enthusiasts for the purpose of regenerating society perhaps the most misunderstood is anarchy. And the reason of this misunderstanding is not far to seek. The average man is not particularly enthusi- astic about governmental institutions, yet in a vague way he is conscious of the stability and comparative freedom which seem to depend on then), and so he supports sovereign authority. The conception of anarchy he gets from newspapers and other sources as being simply the destruction of all officials and the reduction of society to chaos, is so firmly established in his mind that the anarchist is condemned out of hand as a lunatic, a destroyer of society and a species of human vermin. In his mind, anarchy, bomb, destruction and chaos are words of similar meanings. In vain it is pointed out that the vast majority of anarchists are peaceable men and good citizens, that some of the world's noblest characters have been anarchists, and that where there is sacrifice there must necessarily be pure motives; he remains obdurate, and , the anarchist to him is still a violent maniac bent on satisfying an instinct for destruction. Concerning every movement originated for the purpose of reforming society several things may be taken for granted. In the first place there must be sacrifice—that is the giving up of ease and comfort for the sake of an ideal. Secondly the ideal must embody many of the things which are recognised as beautiful and desirable by the great majority of men. Thirdly the methods by which it is sought to attain the ideal will be various and, in varying degrees, imperfect. Finally all reformers are misunderstood both as to their ideals and their methods. It remains now to consider anarchy under the foregoing classification of attributes. The amount of sacrifice is an indication of the intensity of emotion and the zeal of the adherents to a cause. The ideal shows their power of imagination and critical faculties, while their methods demonstrate their constructional or executive ability. That the sacrifice of anarchists is great admits ot no argument. Russia alone has furnished thousands of martyrs to the anarchist ideal. Men and women have faced and are facing death and imprisonment for the sake of the teaching which they believe will regenerate society. The anarchist ideal is much misunderstood' Arche is from a Greek word meaning power or rule. Archbishop is d ruling bishop. Hept- archy was a government of seven persons. Oligarchy means government by a few selected rulers.

Now Anarchy is the ideal of those who contemplate a state of society in which there are no rulers or governors. It does not imply that there is to be no order or organisation, but that these are to be obtained by agreement instead of by coercion. Self-discipline is to take the place of external discipline and every individual is to be free to do as he pleases excepting in so far as his action interferes with the similar freedom of others. It is thus evident that anarchy could only obtain among a highly cultured people where the will of the units was always i harmony with the will of the whole, and also that the environment of the individuals would tend to produce a still higher type. We approach nearest to a state of anarchy in our social intercourse. A number of friends seated round a table •are orderly and still there is no coercion except opinion. They do not fight and grab for food, because each knows that there is enough for all and each has confidence that the rest will see that he does not go short. Anarchy, or the absence of a person or class in authority, is thus seen to be a relation among men which springs from mutual confidence and esteem. The ideal of democracy is not very dissimilar. Democracy means a state in which the people agree on some common action and appoint certain of their number to carry it out. In democracy the people would be so enlightened that they would determine the laws instead of merely appointing the law-makers. That is to say, democracy as well as anarchy contemplates a state of society in which there shall be no rulers. With the ideals of anarchy every cultured man must be in sympathy. A condition of society in which no-one had desires contrary to the welfare of the community would certainly make a delightful world. But is it possible ? Is human nature in the mass capable of attaining such a high moral standard? Evidently anarchists have answered these questions affirmatively. It remains now for us to consider the means by which they hope to approach their ideal. Although violent anarchists are few in numbers, comparatively speaking, they have, by their spectacular methods attracted by far the most attention. The violent anarchist sees misery and suffering by the side of arrogance and extravagance, and concludes that by removing the latter he will also remove the former. His motives are pure, his sacrifice is real, but his intellectual facul- ties are of a low order. Otherwise he would realise that the government of a country is always representative, and that the extrava- gance of the rich results from the ignorance of the poor. He would have reached the conclusion, held by the world's greatest thinkers, that political institutions are the outcome of individual character and culture. The philosophic anarchist to a certain degree realises this truth and consequently his line of action is different. He preaches the anarchist ideal and endeavours to make converts, at the same time passively sub- mitting to authority as being an unavoidable evil. He is logical in arguing that a state of anarchy can only be attained by making anarchists. But does his philosophy provide for the education and training which will be required for the development of higher individual character? Does he realise that self-disciplined individuals in large numbers can only be obtained by means of a suitable environment ? It is one thing to desire anarchy and another thing to be fit for it. Practical anarchists are few, and so far their efforts have not been particularly successful, but all will agree that they are moving in the right direction. A 'group of them acquire land and machinery and set up a state of things in which there is no ruler— organisation is by agreement. In this way, it is argued, anarchists themselves will be trained in their own principles, and they cannot be misunderstood by the average observer. If small groups of people can live in a state of anarchy, eventually all can thus live, as the necessary high level of individual character is reached. W. BAGULEY.

TEA FROM INDIA.

']be Britibh housewife is a devotee of the tea •cnp and every year shows an increasing consumption of tea by the inhabitants of the United Kingdom. One of the mot satisfactory features of this increase in the con- sumption of tea is that the greater part of it comes from our own dominions. The Empire of India, of the tea from which we consumed Dst year 169,000,0001b5., has continued during this year to supply us with by far the greater port of our requirements. During April of this year we consumed 15,000,000 lbs. of Indian tea, being o'er 2,000,000 more than we consumed in April, 1911, whilst in the four months which ended with the 30th of April we consumed over 56,0t0.0001b5. of India* tea, over 3,000,000 more than in the corresponding period of last year. These figures, which are taken from tbe official report of the Board of Trade, are sufficient evidence of our appreciation of the sterling value of the dQlicious tea supplied us by our fellow subjects in India.

DR. BARNARDO'S HOMES. Garden Fete at Latchford.

The members of the Young Helpers' League could hardly have been more unfortunate in the day chosen for their garden lete and sale of work in aid of Dr. Barnardo's Homes. Tuesday produced some of the most depressing weather which even this extraordinary summer has seen and it required more than average optimism to enter upon an al fresco function under such conditions. Councillor and Mrs. Jackson Holmes kindly placed the grisunda at Barnacre, Latchford Without, at the disposal of the League and an interesting programme of games and other amusements had been arranged. However, the frequent heavy showers made it impossible to carry out everything in the way intended, but some of the entertainments were given in the house and proved highly enjoyable. The sale of work was held in a marquee, the stallholders being Mrs Woodcock, Miss D Truslove, Miss Willett, Mrs Bedell, and Miss Newall. Miss Pattie Woodcock had charge of the bran tub. An orchestra, under the leadership of Mrs. Willett, submitted some choice selections. Many of the visitors availed themselves of the opportunity of having their hands " read " by a lady palmist. Interesting entertainments arranged by Miss Bicadbent included tongs by Mrs Briggs, a monologue "1 and my father-in-law" by Miss Elsie Lee Jones, and a duologue "Contradictions" by Miss Broadbent and Miss M Broadbent, each item being very creditably performed and well received. Special acknowledgment must be wade of the invaluable services of Mrs Willett the hon treasurer, and Mrs W II Woodcock the hon. secretary, who were enthusiasti- cally backed up by Mrs Ilo'ves and other helpers. The fete was opened by Mrs Flidgway, of Wildersmocr, Lymtn, in the presence of a fairly largo gathering. Mrs Rylands, of Massey Hall, presided. Mrs. Ridgway said it bad been said that there was nothing more precious Cr more beautiful than youth, and no-one would dispute the attractive charm, the delicious freshness and the fragrant poesibilites that belonged so essentially to anything that was young. But might they not go one step further and say that nothing could be more beautiful than youth wben it was dedicated to the service of those who suffered, who were in dire need, and who were in such terribly sad circumstances that no friend or help was near. She used not speak much of Dr.Barnardo's homes or of the wonderful work that was dune there and the keenness and enthusiasm which to-day was just as great as it was in those first marvellous days. All of those present were interested in that great and good work f. r the little ones who were born amid such awful scenes of sin and sorrow. Last Saturday was the Founder's Day. It was cele- brated at the Girls' Villege Homes at Barkingside, Essex, and to a great and v3mpatlittic gathering Mr. Baker spoke of bow greatly the homes were in dtbt owing to two years' deficits, principally duo to the fact that very few legacies had recently been left them .£30,C00 were urgently needed for the work of the homes. Altogether 21,1)00 children had been sent to Canada and leas than 2 per cent. had gone wrong—a splendid tribute to their training and care. (Applause). In 1911, 5u trained lads left home for a sea faring career. Twenty-one entered tie Navy and one went down with the Titanic. On Saturday also was inaugurated the Australasian hospital, built as a tribute to the memory of Dr. Bartardo, hi gifts collected by sympathetic friends in Australia and New Zealand. Ihe hospital had three pavilions and accommodated 66 children. Perhaps the most striking fact of the great work of Dr. Barnardo's institutions was that no child had ever been refused help. The door was always wide open. The divine figure of charity, whose other name was love, was always standing there with widespread arms ready to enfold those little ones whose previous possession of youth had had no opportunity of blossom- ing into a thing of beauty. God's grace had been abundantly poured on the efforts of those noble, Christ-like, self-sacrificing men and women who had given their lives for others and to-day, all over the world there, were men and women who :thanked God with every fibre of their being for having been brought in their hour- of dire necessity to the shelter of Dr. Barnardo's homes and the influence and training of those noble workers. " Therefore," concluded Mrs. Ridgway, " shall we not to-day, despite the unkind and gloomy skies—they ought to beam with shining joy on such an effort—do our best to further the earnest endeavour of those young helpers who have inaugurated the sale and fete ? Let us show by our sympathy, not only passive but truly active, that we are indeed one with them in their efforts to do what little they can, and so shall we ourselves have given our mite to this great and noble work of love which must be very dear to the heart of the Master Who once said: 'Suffer the little children to come unto Me, for of such Ii the Kingdom of Heaven.' " (Applause). Mrs. Itylands, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs. Ridgway, said they all heartily sympathised with the work of Dr. 13sruardo's institutions. Councillor Jackson Holmes soconded and the vote was cordially passed. Mrs. Jackson Holmes was also thanked for her kind interest in the fete. It is expected that as a result of the fee about £l.O will be handed over to the Hones.

Y/OMEN'S GHAT.

The Salvation Army Chief.—General Booth, despite his illness and the loss of his sight, still proposes to visit the United States and Canada, As he intended. Like Beethoven who wrote his finest music after he had become stone deaf, so the venerable chief of the Salvation Army, hopes to be able to go on with his work. He is 83 years of age, but Ike Dr Clifford, Tolatoy, and Gladstone, General Booth is a monument of what may be accomplished by the simple life. Vegetarians, teetotalers and non-smokers are all entitled to claim him as a splendid example of the virtues of their respective theories of abstinence, and bis wonderful vitality is the more remarkable bee arse, as a lad, be had very delicate health, acd was told he bad not the strength ti undertake Wesleyan Mission work. The Salvation Army hes its critics, but the world acknowledges in General Booth, an historical figure, whose life is SS wonderful as his work, and one of the greatest organisers the world has ever coen. The Handicap of Blindness.— Of all the crushing blows that fall upon flesh and blood,that which dep ices a person of sight, is one of the greatest, not yithstand- ing the progress that has been n ade in the training of the blind to enable them to Isad useful lives. It is said that a woman feels the affliction mere than a man. Although they may acquire many wonderful talents by the 23011E03 of touch and hearing, the blind are very conscious of their infirmity, and never forget it. Nothing can give to them that full delight in I eautiful things, which a won an, more than a man Craves forond which no word pictures can entirely satisfy, lint a new aid is promirel them— an electric ,I substitute for the human eye,abich will enable them to reeognise olskcts by means of light transformed into Found. Pictures, as we know, can be rent by wire,but of all "the fairy tales of science "the most wonderful is that of Mr.Fournier's hopeful promise, of a substituted sense for the blind. He even believes that it may be possible to so far perfect the invention, as to enable the blind to read print and to do practically all that the sighted can do. A Good Deal in a Name.—Fabulous prices are often paid for old silverware, not because it has any merits in art or workmanship, which cannot be (quailed in the present day, but simply because it is oiCi. It would seem to he the same with violins. The Italians were, in olden times, considered i uperior to all others, as makers of these instruments. Often as much as £2,000 has been given for a Stradivarius, and almost vs much for a Guarneri, but according to a test recently con- ducted in Paris, they have no superior merits as music II instruments, compered with those of the best modern productions. Experts were invited to listen to the playing of various violins concealed behind a screen. The icstrumen's included a Stradivarius valued at £3,200, a Guarcerius den Jean valued at £2,000 a 1911 French violin, a d a 1912 Belgian instiument. After the violinist had played on orch in turn, the jury awarded the place of honour to the flolgian fiddle, the second place to the French violin, and the third to the £3,200 instrument. The result seems to be one exposure the more—that is, from the ultilitarian point of view. The Summer Sales.—There was a time when it was considered amusing to make fun of the bargain-hunters, who are once n.ore on the war-path, at the stlMlller ash B. The Height cf the Skirt.—Much discussion hal centred of late as to the correct length and height of this season's skirts. The f•irmer may he said to be settled, for it has been decided that " jupos " are to be somewhat longer then last rummer, and that with regard to the latter,both the high and the normal waist are fashionable. The normal, however, has never really gone out of fashion, and in all probability nsver will. It is mush mare serviceable than the high, which has a tendency to roll over somewhat at the top, and thus to lose the smart appearance which its rival rets;es for a much longer period. It therefore appeals to the business woman and to others who may buy their clothes with an eye to their durability and well-wearing qualities. MR. HENINIERDE ON THE GOVERNMENT'S POLICY. OPPOSITION'S " HORRIBLE SUSPICION."

Mr. G. llemmerde, M.P., speaking at a Liberal girden party at Highgate on Saturday night, said the Tory party were rather disturbed about the Liberal land proposals. They had got hold of the information that there wai a committee at work on these questions, of which Mr. Lloyd George was the inspiring spirit, and of which he (Mr. Hemmerde) was one of the members. Their opponents had a horrible suspicion that the relit y which upset their hopes in North-west Norfolk might upset their hopes still more lamentably at the next general election ; and he thought it would. tie did not fight North-west Norfolk without knowing that powerful forces wore ready to take up the taxation of land values, and he predicted that this was the question upon which the next general election would be fought.

CO-OPERATORS AND THE INSURANCE ACT.

Meeting of the Local Society.

The Co-operative Wholesale Society has formed a section under the National Insurance Act and is now one of the many approved scmieties in the Kingdom. Under the auspices of the Warrington Branch of the society a meeting at which there was a fur attendance was held in the Co-operative halt on Monday everung. Mr. J. Ja-man presided and Mr. R. L. Williams, of the C.W.S. National Insurance Section, delivered an address on " Co-operators and the National Insurance Act," explainiul the position of members of the Co-operative movemen,. in connection with this great men sure.

At the outset the CHAIRMAN said that the niee'ing was held entirely under non-political ausp'ces, and pointed out that Mr. Williams would be prepared to answer any questions that night that might be put to him. There were a number of approved societies of one sort tad another administering the Act, and amongst others the Co-eperative Wholesale Society had decided to firm a section in connection with the bueinets in Manchester, end work it through their various branch es throughout the country who would not as their agents.- So far as that was concerned they were in a very good position. They had offices and braccees belonging to the members, and they were there to do all they could for the benefit of the members. They were there not in the interests of any section of the community, but in the interests of all the members, and if they wished to join this particular society as their approved society he could assure them they would get the beat pessible value for their money. The committee had felt that there were a number of people in the tows, including many young women who were net connected with any Trade Union or Friendly Seciety and who seemed to be in consider- able difficulties :s to what to do. The men knew what to do but the girls were entering the scheme for the first time and were at a loss to know how test to act The Co-operative officials would give the membe information au jut other Friendly Societies if they desired. This society did not waut to run a competi- tion against friendly societies or Trade Unione but they desired to give their members an opportunity of join- ing their section if they wanted to do to. (Applause.) Mr. Wteeteeis said the officials of the C.W.S. had been watching the progress of the Bill and if they had thought anything detrimental to the members had been going forward, they would have taken steps to defend themselves. The directors of the C.W.S National Insurance s.ction were advised that, under sections 23 and 24 of the Act, it would be possible to form an orgenieation such is theirs into an insurance section for the benefit of co-operators throughout the country, I administered by their own committee, having no necessity for separate balance sheets or alteration of rules. It was found that by becoming an ii surnnee section they had every advantage and no disadvantage as oompared with an approved society. In deciding upon that course, the C.W.S. were very anxious that it should ha made known that they were not acting against , the interests of their old friends—the trade unionists 1 and the friendly a cieties. The co-operative cause owed too much to them for any of the directors or leaders of their mivemeat to do anything againet their interests. (Applause.) Centinuine, the speaker said he thought they would agree with him that the co-operative movement which had done so much for education and the general welfare of the people of the country had taken the best and wisest course in becoming an approval society. An able and interesting definition of the general principles of the Act was then given by the speaker, after which he said the chief work in connection with their society would be in the direction of sickness. For the first time co-operators must realize that in that matter they were entering upon the duties of ordinary friendly societies. The local committee would have to receive reports as to the number of sick cases in their district sod pay over the amounts to the stores on receipt of the voucher which would be issued by the authorities under the Insurance Committee of the town. When that voucher was presented the amount and the book would be sent to Manchester and it would rank as credit for cash. Ile was pleased to say that on Saturday the oflioial seal of the Commissioners was put to the docu- ment, which made their section an appreved society, and they would be able to place contribution cards to the local stores throughout the country during that week and the members would get them as soon as possible. (Applause.) Wben insured persons received the cards they bad to hand them to their truployers, upon whom it would be incumbent to place stamps on the cards to the value of 7d for males and 6d. for females. At the end of the quarter it would be the employers duty to hand the card to the empleyee who, would take it into hi 3 organization, in their own Case, the stores, and the cards would be collected and sent to the headquareers of their organization in Manchester, and another blank card would be issued. There would he one set of benefits throughout the country, and it would he possible for a co-operator to remove from Manchester to Sheffield, from Newcastle to London' from London to Nottingham, and other places "rah absolutele no change of benefit and with less inconvenience than would be found in ocionectlon with any other otganizision in the country. (Applause.) It was necessary in matters of th's kind, when so much was at stake, that care should be taken in the selection of the men they entrusted with the management of the finance, and in that respect there was no organleatson throughout thcecountry which cculd present such a solid and substantial advantage as the Co-operative Wholesale Society. (hear, hear) They in War- rington had their voice in the management of the Wholesale Society, as it was managed by a committee elected by the various societies. With the tremendous resources the Cc-operative movement had, they felt justified in seyiug that no one could give a better security than their society. (Applause). The work I could be done more cheaply end he asked them all to band together c fficiently and administer ably and well the advantages of the Act to their own people. The accounts of the society had been audited by able accountants and they were found to be in a position whereby they would work the Act at a rate of Is. Bd. per member per year. (Applause). Their society being able to work at such a cheap rate could offer supe ior benefits. (Applaese). They were able to work the scheme at such a low rate because they were going to give it the soundest and mast so'entifie management they could get. It was plainly evi tent that the more a socdtty was able to centralise and specialise an indue'ry or scheme tbe more they were able to minimise the cost. (Applau Questions were then invited and the first one was : "Can a woma3 who is an out worker and who dws net depend on out work for a livelihood but whose husband is able to keep her, be exempt nom the Act ? " Mr. WILLIAMS slid that if an out worker had a , husband who was able to keep her and if it was shown that he could keep her, ehe could secure a certificate of- exemption. Teen the persona who employed her would loom to cbtain from the Post Office an exemption I stamp and pay the threepence. ' QUESTIONF.R : Will the husband have to pay the extra threepence ? Mr. Wteteeets : The husband not being the employer will not have to pay anything. The pereon for whom it is piid will derive no benefit whatever. , Other questions were asked and satisfactorily answered by the speaeer. In proposing a vote of thanks to the speaker, Mr. E. ENGLAND said they had experienced no difficulty in understanding the explanation of the Act by Mr. Williams. lie was sure that tho Act would be a bene- ficial one. The groat point for the meeting to consider was what approved society they should belong to, and from which the greatest benefits could be obtained. They had teen told that their own society could be worked at the rate of Is. Bd. ahead, That was a r mark- able and startling fact, because it had been found thathe general cost in connection with the friendly societies was 4s. Id. (Applause). THE LEADING TARIFF REFORM AUTHORITY ON GERMANY, Mr. J. Ellis Barker, had to say in his article in the " Nineternth Century " for June, on "The Failure of Post-Ilismarckian Germany." lie wrote : " Not only politically and militarily, but economically also has Germany lost ground, especially during the last few years. During the last few sears German imperial, net ional, and local expenditure has increased at an unheard-of rate, and the expenditure has been provided for partly out of taxes and partly out of loans. The following figures are significant : AMOUNT OF GERMAN IMPERIAL DART. 1888 1890 Os ing to a simultaneous great increase in taxation the cost of production has risen so much that many industries ehich produce goods that riquire much labour have begun to suffer. The finer productions require much, the coarser little, labour. Apart from machinery the increases (in German exports) have been particularly great in coarser manufactures, such as raw iron, cowse iron-ware, steel rails, &0., in which the labour cost is proportionately small. On the other hand there have been during the same time very considerable decreases in the exports of cotton and woollen goods, clothing, fine ironware, gold and silver wire, books, colour prints, porcelain, &c , in all of which the cost of labour is proportionally great. The fact that German industry is no longer pro- gressing as rapidly as it used to, and that it is arparehtly approaching the point where stagnation begins, is particularly noticeable in the shipbuild- ing and shipping industries. The new German Tariff is far too elaborate for practical purposes. Already great harm has been done to the national Industries. German taxation, like the German tariff, suffers from over-elaboration in all its branches. . . The fundamental principle of taxation, that the cost of collection should be small in proportion ti the produce of the tax, has been forgotten. Germany, which used to be the best governed, is now merely the most governed country in th i e world, and the defects of the Government n all its branches have created general dissatistsotirn. Of that dissatisfaction the rapid ig ro wth of the Social Democratic Party is the most noteworthy symptom. Germany is politically, militarily, economically, administratively, and morally on the down grade." If the German tariff way far too elaborate, what about the tariff they were trying to put on here-2). a qnerter on wheat, 5 per cent on dairy produce, and 10 per cent on the average on manufactured articles, meaning that some would get 2 per cent in order theit others might get 18 par cent. The system Interfered not only with the liberty of the rnbject but with the processes of manufacture. Let Mr. Harold Smith put Mr. Ellis Barker's statement in his pipe and smoke it, and let him contrast it with the message he sent. (Applause). Sometimes the tariff reformers tried to disguise the fact that that they were

FOOD TAXERS, but it must never be forgotten that Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, in the House of Commons on May 28 b, 1903, declared : "If you are to give preference to the Colonies you must put a tax on food." He further added : " Now this policy, if it is carried out, will cost you so much a week more than you are paying at preaent for your food" and " It is the calculation in all taxes on consumption that the poorer classes pay three. fourths and the well-to-do one fourth." But even when food was made dearer, they had an important tariff reformer's authority for saying that the Increased price would not fled its way into the pockets of the labourer or of the farmer. Mr. Jesse Collings, in a speech at York, on February 130, 1908 said : " If they were to give him a 10s. duty on every quarter of wheat he raised, what would happen ? In a very short time that 10s. would appear in the rent. It always bad been so." Mr. rjollmga in a letter to a Nottingham newspaper, further told them : "The farmer Cannot grow corn in face of the competition of the whole world. If pro- tection were given him—and nothing short of Os. or Bs. a quarter would be of any u-e—it would, I fear, be of no use to the tenant farmer." In a previous speech at Warrington he (Mr. Gleave) attributed the Tariff Reform propaganda very largely to the influence of the Yankee hustler and trust monger and of the men who whoted to imitate his example. His view had during the last few weeks received striking corroboration from an authority which should appeal to CLnaervatives in Warrington.

LORD IMOD CECIL, In his book " Conservatism "—the most authoritative and able exposition of the philosophy of Conservatism that bad been published in our generation—said : " To the purely Conservative element in modern Conservatism it (tariff reform) does not appeal, because the Conservative, looking back to the years of success and prosperity that have followed the adoption of Free Trade, is indisposed to make a change. Nor is it difficult for him to see that Tariff Reform might easily lead to a more decidedly Protectionist system, aud that anything like high protection in this country would smooth the way for Socialism. For under a Protectionist system the growth of trade combinations and trusts would he greatly stimulated es in America and Germany ; and it would be very difficult to argue that it was more dangerous and mischievo us to place all the means of production in the hands of the State itself than to have them monopolised by a number of private combinations."

OPEN-AIR POLITICS,

MR HAROLD SMITH'S VISIT TO GERMANY.

What a Tariff Reform Authority Says of German Trade Stagnation.

A striking address on the fircal question, with special reference to Mr. Harold Smith's picture of German prosperity, was delivered by Mr. Samuel Gleave, of the Free Trade Union, at the open-air meeting held by the Pe, greasive Labour League near Arpley Cannons on Monday evening. There was a large and enthusiastic gathering. Mr. lIARRY NEW, who presided, said it seemed like carrying coals to Newcastle to hold meetings to con- vince intelligent audiences 89 to the merits of Free Trade as compared eith Tariff Reform. Believing in the justice of their cause, however, they felt It their brooder' duty to take up the challenge of the other side who sought at every oppor- tunity to rnir-represent the true state of affairr. Having referred to the shifting attitude of the Tories on the question, m exemplified in the Holmfirth election, Mr New said the Liberals were constantly charged with hypo( risy because they bad not taken off all the taxes on food. It was qnite tree that the food of this country was taxed to the extent of nearly £9,750,000, but during the last six years the Liberal Government, by reducing the tea tax one penny per lb., the sugar tax from 4a 2d to Is 10d and the cocoa tax one penny, had practically saved the country £7,000,000 in food taxes. Had the Tories been returned to power, it ' was their avowed intention to levy taxes on foreign wheat, meat, dairy produce and manufactured goods, but not on raw ma'erial. The man was not breathing to-day who could define what raw material was. It would be well to call the Tariff Reform League's attention to the fact that during the last sixty years no Liberal Government had ever Increased the taxes en the food or clothing of the people by one farthing. (Applause.) Under Free Trade the Liber,l Government had been able to reduce the national debt by .C56,C00,000 —which meant a clear saving to the taxpayers of .C 2,000,000 a year—to grant old age pensions, to set aside sums for afforestation and the fOnnding of satiated& for consumptives, and to bring in Budgets 'which resulted in the magtaificent surplus of £6,000,000, (Applause). Mr. S. GLEAVE strongly empbadsed the fundamental importance of the Free Trade question and pointed out out that all the advantages which the people gained from the social legislation of the present Government would be jeopardised if one of two things happened—if we became involved in a European war or if We ellowed tariff reform to be enacted. The introduc- tion of tariff reform would be even more grave than the launching of this country upon a European war, because even in the care of the biggest war, we could slowly extricate ourselves from the bag into which it had driven us, and by careful industry and national frugality, restore the credit and prosperity of the country. But Tariff Reform was not so easy thing to get rid of. lhis country surely found that out in the long agitation which Wee necessary to bring about the repeal of the G,rn Laws. Once those Corn Laws were re-enacted the task would be even more difficult than it was before. In the presidential elec- tion now in progress in America, all the candidates were saying they stood for a reduction of the tariff. The revolt there was so serious that they all recognized that there was not the slightest chance of their getting the votes of the electorate unless they could make , them believe that they could do something to rid I them of the shackles of Protection. The same applied to Germany, Italy and France. The Tariff Reformers said : "If the Germans do not like Protection why don't they get rid of it?" They might as well say with regard to a rat in a trap, " Wby does'nt it get out of it, it It does'nt like it" ? (Laughter). Tariff Reform was the only alternative to the policy of the present Government, for the

TORY PARTY DAD BEEN CAPTURED AND NOBBLED by the tariff reformers, although the prop'e of this country had shown time after time that they did not want tariff reform. He (the speaker) did not believe that the electors of Warrington sent Mr. Harold Smith to Parliament to vote for tariff reform. A large number of them thought there was no serious danger of tariff reform ; they were thinking about other things and neglecting that, vital one. If the Tory party really thought that the sending of Mr. Smith to Parliament meant that the electors of Warrington were in favour of tariff reform, Oey would not have the Tariff Reform League holding weekly meetings and going round into the wards whipping up every supporter and bringing forward time after time their so-called rt,guments and statistics with a view to reconciling the people to their policy. It seemed to indicate that even if the Tories thought the electors of Warrington were in favour of tariff reform at the last election, they were by no means sure that they were still in favour of it. (Laughter.) The Tories were talking a great deal at present about social reform, and as Mr. F. E. Smith was especially prominent in that direc- tion they would doubtless be hearing something in the same strain from Mr. Harold Smith who was an echo— though a rather weak one—of his brother. (Laughter.) Notwithstanding all that talk, however, the Tories I admitted in their candid moments that the real thing for all practical purposes was tariff reform. Mr. F. E. Smith, speaking at Cardiff on June 3rd, said :- 1 " I speak with knowledge when I say there is I not, end there never will be. a question of the Unionibt Party dropping Tariff Reform. We shall either form a Government which is pledged and able to deal with Tariff Reform or we shall not form a Government at all." Therefore, if at the next election Mr. Harold Smith tried to

MINIMISE TII9 ISSUE and to lay that the important question was the Insur- ance Bill, Home Rule or Welsh Disestablishment, let them sok him if his brother had not declared that the most important thing of all—practically the only thing that mattered—was tariff reform. Professor Hewing, M.P„ also speaking at Cardiff on June 3rd, declared : " The progress of the Tariff Reform movement had been absolutely continuous since Mr. Chamber- lain began bis campaign. The moment the Con- servative party was in power it would come forward with this policy, to which it vas committed." Even more weighty was the utterance of Mr. Austen Chamberlain who, from all appearances, was the man who would be charged with putting Into effect the first tariff reform Budget. Speaking at the annmtl meeting of the Tariff Reform League on November &b, 1911, he said : Everyone knew what they would do when they were returned to power ; and without any further mandate, sanction, or approbation, the moment a Unionist Government was returned, it would set about putting Tariff Reform and Imperial Prefer- ence principles into statutory form and place them without any delay or any qualification on the Statute Book."

Padgate.

WESLEY GITILD.—The monthly devotional meeting of the Guild was held on Monday evening. Rev E Morgan presided and on behalf the local Guild welcomed as visitors the members of the Bewsev-road Guild who attended. The lesson was read by Mr C Morgan and the solo "Nearer my God to Thee" was sung with feeling by Miss B London. The chief event of the evening was however, the presence of the Rev Thomas Gladding (formerly pastor of the church and now of Macclesfield). He received a warm welcome and gave a most thoughtful and helpful address. The subject was " The fourfold call of Christ," and the rev, gentle- man based his remarks on the following four divisions of the call : " Come unto Me," "Learn of Me," " Follow Me "and "Abide in Me." After the lecture a collection was taken on behalf of the Guild funds and the roll was called as usual. Ors the motion of Mr Arthur Bennett,J.P., seconded by Mr W Healey (who in the course of his remarks extended a cordial invitation to the members of the Padgate Guild•to visit Bewsey- road Guild at an early date), a hearty and unanimous vote of thanks was passed to Mr Gladding to which he;suil ably replied.

A PENKETH WEDDING.

Photo Bates.

A GROUP AFTER THE CEREMONY. The wedding took place on Mouday afternoon last at 2 o'clock in the Wesleyan Chapel, of Mr. Hugh McMillan Dewar, of Blantyre, Scotland, and Miss Sarah Jane (Jenny) W olverston, who has been a resident of Penketh for some years and had made very many friends. The bride was given away by Mr. J. W. Baker, butcher, Warrington-road, Penketh. The bridegroom's brother (Mr. John Dewar) acted as best man. The bridesmaid was Miss Dolly Baker. The Rev. J. Gunnell officiated at the marriage ceremony, and Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., officiated at the organ. A reception was afterwards held in the Wesleyan schoolroom, when over SO guests were present. The refresh- ments were in the capable haLds of Mesdames T. Bell, T. Dunabin and J. Foden. The newly- married couple left in the evening for Blackpool where the honeymoon will be spent. The bride and bridegroom were the recipients of a large number of handsome presents. The guests, who enjoyed themselves thoroughly, kept up the event till late in the evening with games and dancing.

Grappenhall.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.

The monthly meeting of the Grappenhall Parish Council was held in the Council Schools on Wednesday evening. Mr Lister-Kaye presided and there were also present :—Messrs J Bradford, F Ellison, F Fox, F Burrows, J Carter, W Lomas and Charles H Hoult (clerk). There was very little business to be transacted. Mr Alfred Garside was elected to fill the vacancy caused by the resignation of Dr Hutt. THE SEWERAGE SCHEME. In accordance with a resolution passed by the Parish Council to oppose the sewerage scheme. Die Council decided to continue operations in the hope of getting a less costly and more modified scheme. In order that the work may be kept on the move, Mr A Lister-Kaye and Mr John Bradford were appointed as a sub- committee for the purpose.

MONTHLY MEETINGS DISPENSED WITH.

It was decided tbat in future the meetings be held quarterly instead of monthly.

Stockton Heath.

ST. THOMAS' BAND 43E HorE.—The first of a series of open air Band of Hope meetings was held on 'Thurs- day evening in Derby-road in which the following took part : Mr. Hallows, Mr. and Mrs. Whiteman, Miss Baguley and Mr. Hatton. A number of recitations were given by the members. Altogether a very pleasant evening was spent. ST. THOMAS' MEN'S CLASS (SECTION B).—About 80 membeis and friends left the village the other morning in brakes and spent an enjoyable time at Norley. The party spent the afternoon in various ways, cricket and rounders being the most popular amusements. Dancing was also indulged in, while others found pleasure in wandering through the forest, returning laden with wild flowers. After te,t, on the motion .of Mr. Hallows, votes of thanks were passed to the committee, caterers, and others for their excellent arrangements. The party left for home at about 6.30, via Frodsham.

HILL CLIPEE BAPTIST SUNDAY SCHOOL.—The annual Sunday school trip was held on Friday of last' week when a 'party numbering over 200 was formed, comprising scholars, parents and friends. The journey I was commenced at 1 o'clock for Mr George Kinsey's farm, Lower Stratton, and a most enjoyable time vvas spent in various games. A large company sat down to tea, and were well served by the teachers and lady members of the church. At 8.30 the company assembled around the house and sang " God be with you till we meet again," and the pastor (Rev. George Curtis) pro- posed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Kinsey and family for their great kindness and hospitality which made this visit so enjoyable. Mr. J. Moss, jun., superintendent of the school, seconded and three hearty cheers were then given for Mr Kinsey and family. The party left for home about 9 o'clock, and a very pleasant drive was enjoyed—the children and friends singing various hymns during the journey. Victoria Square was reached about 10 o'clock. The lurries were kindly lent by Messrs. Stanley Carter and Co., Warrington Slate Co., Albinson Bros., Hayes Bros., and Mr J Moss, jun. All were delighted with this most successful outing, which constituted a record.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Pilkington Bros., protesting against • the Council's action in deciding to issue a closing order on certain property in Gas-street, owned by them, and set- ting forth the alterations they were prepared to make to h property to put it in thorough repair. 1%.r Gosh" moved the confirmatoon of the minutes, the after first objecting to the letter being read.
