RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr C E Linaker presiding.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
I%7IEDIC AL OFFICER,

The Clerk read a letter from the Local Government Board stating that they approved of the appointment of Dr J H Nicholl as deputy medical officer of health for the Runcorn Council during the absence, on leave, of Dr J Adams. .

Dr Adams, writing from the Nelson House Nursing Home, Manchester, said his operation bad been performed a week ago and was of much greater severity than was anticipated. Some little time would be necessary for canvalescence but if all went well he would be able to attend the next meeting of the Council. He desired to convey his thanks for the Council's consideration and 53mp5thy in his illness. He sincerely hoped the die- trict would not become affected with pleuro-pneumonia. The Chairman said they all hoped the doctor would be quite restored to health when he did return. (Hear, hear).

===== 0002601_19120706_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INSURANCE ACT.

At a meeting of the Parliamentary Committee it was resolved that the employees of the Council receiving salaries of less than £l6O per year be insured under part one of the Act and that those employees carrying out duties within the meaning of part two of the Ant be insured against unemploymtnt. Mr Pilling moved the adoption of the minute and the Chairman said the committee had come to a very right conolusion. As law-abiding people it was their duty to obey the law in the matter, no matter how disagreeable that might be. They could, however, try to get the Act amended if it was thought necessary. Mr. Hallows : What about the road workers of the Council. Have they to be insured? The Chairman and Others: Of course, yes. Mr Hallows said he would like to know bow they were going to take 4d per week off the wages of such poorly paid men ? The Chairman: They will have to be dealt with, within the meaning of the Act.

Mr. Hallows : The men ought to have a wage from which the fourpence could be deducted without handi- capping them. I won't say anything about the Act, or perhaps I might vex someone. Mr Pilling : I hope you won't say that. (Laughter.) Mr Hallows : I think Lloyd George ought to pay It all. (Laughter and cries of "Shame.") The action of the Committee was approved. ALLEGED BAD ROADS.

Mr C L Evans, of Norton Estate, wrote calling the attention of the Council to the state of the roads to Messrs Morris and Percival's farms which was not at all satisfactory. It was decided that Mr Evans be asked to specify which particular road be meant, as the surveyor could not find any cause for complaint. He was also requested to arrange to meet the Surveyor on the spot.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BRINE BILL.

Mr Linaker said that as Chairman of the Council, he felt he must say something with regard to the rejection of ithe Brine Pumping Bill. No one reading the Bill could have come to any other conclusion but that the committee would throw it out. The provisions of the Bill were attempting to put a restraint on trade and it was very much against public polioy to pass such a proposal. Be thought the promotion of the Bill by the County Council had been condemned by all right thinking people and Sir John Brunner had given expression to his feelings in the words "It is a waste of public money." They had been told by the Highways authority of the County Council that they had no money to spend on ro d improvements but if they had spent the money on maintaining the County roads instead of in the Bill, it w )uld have been far better spent. The cost of the Bill, according to Sir John Brunner, was .C 20,000. Chat Council and other authorities had done all they could to prevent the Bill becoming law and they had been successful. It would have been a very serious matter for that district if the Bill had been passed for, generally speaking, the district would have been crippled if in the future brine had been found within the three-mile limit, and they had not had the facilities for economical salt manufactur- ing. It would have been valuable property locked up. His feeling was strong on the matter, and he thought they could flatter themselves that they did their best to help to deflat such a very unjust Bill. (Hear, hear and applause). THE MEDICAL orricEß's REPORT.

Dr Nrcnoll reported one case of typhoid from Weston and said the necessary precautions had been taken. The report was adopted. THE CATTLE PLAGUE. On the conclusion of the general business of the Council Mr. Roylance said the foot and mouth disease outbreak amongst cattle as a very serious matter and affected agriculturists very much—in fact it would go so far as to ruin some of them as it did long ago. He thought the District Council should take the matter into consideration at once because of the serious position the farmers were placed in. He suggested that a resolution should be sent to the members of Parlia- ment and the Prime Minister to see if some assistance could be given to the farmers. It would be a very serious matter if they lost their stocks and they had nothing else to fall back upon, as was the case on the last occasion of such an outbreak. l'hey should try and bring powers to bear on members of Parliament to help the farmers and he thought the Board of Agriculture would help them in some way. Mr. Woodward pointed out that compensation was paid to those farmers who had cattle destroyed. Mr. Linaker : I think it is a little bit premature. (Hear, hear). Mr. Roylance : It is no use locking the door when the horse has been stolen. Several members pointed out that compensation was paid to the farmers who had cattle destroyed and the matter was not further discussed.

The Examiner, Saturday, July, 6, 1912.

Letters were read from Lord Wolmer and from Mr E M Lacey, on the subject of the electric lighting order,and it was resolved that they be referred to the Electio Lighting Sub-Committee for consideration. LORD VVOLMER'S STRONG LANGUAGE. , Speaking at an Anti-Disestablishment demonstration lat Cardiff on Saturday, Viscount Wollner, M.P., said I Englishmen would expend their last breath before they I would let the venerable and sacred Welsh dioceses go. That demonstration that day and the one in London bad sealed the doom of the accursed bill. They were fight- ing the battle for Christianity, and that was the reason they had the support of thousands upon thousands of Nonconformists. There was no demand for this wicked and irreligious proposal, which would have never seen the light hut for the bitter hatred, the festering hatred, that lay in the cankered minds of a group of politicians who controlled the machinery of the Liberal party, in this manufactured agitation their opponents could only invent arguments and quote documents which were well-known forgeries. In the name of Heaven, he asked, shall Parliament interfere and tear down the glorious work of the Church ? The poor would suffer if this mean Bill succeeded. To their Nonconformist friends he would say : "Go on, do your good work, there's room for all in Wales ; only leave us alone and let us go on with our work." In conrAusion, he said that they would not see their ancient Church spoiled, disgraced, and robbed by this damnable Bill. A resolution condemnatory of the Welsh Church Bill was carried simultaneously at all the platforms.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr J Spruce presiding.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INSURANCE ACT.

Continuing, the Clerk said the Parliamentary Com- mittee had met on the question of the Insurance Act, and had instructed him to forward to each member information as to how the Act would effect the Board of Guardians, and a list of its officers who would have to be insured under the Act. They would report at the next meeting. APPLICATIONS FOR SPECTACLES. MEMBERS INDIGNANT.

The Clerk to the Runcorn Urban Education Com- mittee wrote forwarding the following resolution passed by the Committee at their meeting on the 28th June : That the clerk write to the Runcorn Guardians and ask if they cam see their way to appoint a sub- committee consisting of the local members of the Board to hear and deal with applications for the supply of spectacles and other requisites in necessitous cases, in order to save the applicants a journey to Dutton. " The Clerk said the Visiting Committee had instructed him to write to the Urban Education Committee pointing out that applicants were not compelled to attend before the Board, but if the relieving officer was satisfied with the cases, the glasses Ztc., were supplied. The idea that the applicants had to attend before the Board was absolutely wrong. Mr. Morris thought Boards of Guardians were blamed for a good many things they were not guilty of. The statement that applicants had to attend the Board bad gone forth, throwing discredit on them. (Hear, hear). lie thought they ought to counteract that report which had gone abroad. The Chairman said the cases got careful consideration from the relieving officer. It was decided that the Board should continne to act as before, and that the clerk be imitructed to advise the Education Committee that there was no necessity for applicants to attend before the Board. MR. BAXTER'S RETURN.

The Chairman said they were all pleased to see Mr. Baxter back from his holiday id America. (Hear, hear). Mr. Baxter said he was glad to get back amongst them. He was no less in love with England through having visited America. ABSENT DEFENDANTS REBUKED. •

For two years prosecutions against Runcorn shop- keepers who open on Sundays have been going on. Gradually the defendants have ceased to appear and have sent one individual with the tines.—On Monday Dr M`Dougall said that for the future when he sat on the Bench he would require the personal attendance of the defendants. He did not know how far their action was an offence against the privilege of solicitors, but this appeared to be on the border line. It was only fair to the Court that the defendants should appear and not make a mockery of the proceedings. So long as the law is what it is it must be respected. THE OPPOSITION TO THE BRINE BILL.

Atr-Run—co-r-n-Urban Council on Tuesday evening, Mr E Imisou. who presided, alluded to the successful opposition to the Brine Bill, and said Runcorn had no desire to triumph over the defeat of her neighbours in the salt district. They had real sympathy with Wins- ford in her misfortune. Runcorn had gone through similar bitter experiences, and they sincerely hoped Winsford's trouble would be short-lived.

After paying a tribute to the value of the evidence given by local gentlemen who attended before the Com- mission, Mr Imison went on to say that expert opinion was that there was a great industrial future before Runcorn, and there was little doubt that many manu- facturers would be attracted to the district, the canal facilities of which were excellent,and where electricity could be ' produced at a low rate and in the same quantity that the Liverpool Corporation used for lighting, tramway, and power purposes. A " breeze " arose at the end of the meeting, Mr R Gibbs contending that the secretary to the Council had attended at the inquiry in London without the consent of the committee. The ratepayers had been put to an expense of £l2 12s that need not have been Incurred.

It was explained that the secretary was added to the deputation at the suggestion of the law clerk, and the Chairman pointed out that it was imperative that a certain amount of latitude should be allowed the law clerk and deputation in deciding as to what evidence would be wanted by the Commission.

ST. HELENS.

ECHOES OF THE COAL STRIKE.

The Elementary Committee of the St. Helens School Authority report that during the coal strike they received donations amounting to £1,114 17s 3d for the feeding of children, and of that £579 5s 4d was expended. The balance of £535 lie lid has been placed at the disposal of the Children's Care Cora- ruittee,with a recommendation that one of the purposes for which the money should be used should be the feed- ing of school children during holidays. The Care Corn- mittee have given instructions for a report to be sub- mitted as to the best means to adopt for the dental treatment of school children,and as to suitable premises for the proposed medical treatment of the children.

- - ST. HELENS VICAR FOR LIVERPOOL. The Rev. Canon Wakeford on Sunday announced to his congregation at St. Margaret's Church, Anneld, that the Rev L J Rich, of St. Helens, would take up the ministry of that church on August 4th. He wished that morning to remind them that there would be no serious change in the conduct of the church, that its work, as well as that of the parish, would be carried on in the same spirit and temper as it had been since the founda- tion of the church forty-two years ago. The Rev Leonard James Rich is well-known and highly respected in the Liverpool diocese, being s son of the late Postmaster of Liverpool. tie was educated at Eel)le College, Oxford, where he took his B.A. degree (second class, modern history) 1885, and M.A. in 1889. All his ministerial life has been spent practically in Liverpool. He was ordained deacon 1886. and priest the following year, by the late Bishop of Liverpool, and from 1886 to 1893 did splendid work as curate of St. Margaret's. For the subsequent five years he was curate at St. Nicholas's Parish Church, where he was greatly esteemed. In 1898 he was promoted to the perpetual curacy of St. Thomas's, Ecoleston, St Helena, where he has done excellent work. Mr Rich, who is a lbachelor, will take with him his two present ministerial colleagues to assist him at St.. Margaret's. ST. HELENS TOWN COUNCIL. REFRESHMEN ' BARS IN PUBLIC PARKS.

The-11.1;370-i—oT.t:Tiefens (Alderman Sir Joseph Beecham) presided at the monthly meeting of the St. Helens Council yesterday. Mr Brooke suggested to the Parks Committee that they should establish a temperance refreshment bar on the Queen's Recreation Ground for the benefit of the people using the bowling greens. If a man left the green to obtain refreshments, when he went back he was charged greenage again. That was quite unfair. Mr Peet said the committee would consider the question, but they had been mot with great objection trom ratepayers in other places where similar arrangements had been suggested. Mr Wareing objected to the minute of the Parlia- mentary Committee which appointed Mr Fox and Mr E H Cozens-Hardy, members of the Provisional Insur- ance Committee for the borough in place of Alderman Pilkington and Aldsrman Martin, who did not wish to serve. At the last meeting he complained that not a single member of the Labour party on the Council had been selected to represent the Council on the Insurance Committee, and he proposed Mr Forshaw. Several members said they were practically promised that if any vacancy arose a Labour representative would be appointed, yet they were proposing two names, one of which was the name of an outsider. Alderman Bishop said that Messrs, Pilkington Bros. bad very large interests at stake. That was an important matter to them, and they ought to be con- sidered. There was not the slightest doubt that the Labour interest on the committee was overwhelming. Mr W Bates suggested that Mr W Pillkington should be put in place or Mr Cozens-Hardy. Mr Wareing said they appeared to want anybody but Mr Forshaw. He propoced that he be put on. , The Mayor said he would have to put that to the meeting if it was insisted on, but there was the sugges- tion that the matter could be arranged. Mr Wareing (after a pause) : I withdraw my amend- ment, and let the thing go back for consideration. (Laughter). The minutes were then passed, with this exception.

ATHLETICS.

LOCAL SUCCESSES. On Saturday last atiNlanchester County Police Sports Herbert Holbrook,off 70 yards,won the three-quarter mile steeplechase in 3 min, 40 4-5 secs. after a stiff finish in which the stamina of the local athlete was apparent. At Atherton, where the track was bad,Chris Vose, off 106 yards, was second about five yards behind the winner who had 58 yards handicap allowance from the local man. Time 4 mins. 254-5 secs. R F Anderton, off 27 'raids, annexed a useful third prize in the 300 yards hurdle. Other recent successes point to very good running by the local athletes notably at Mexborough where J E Cartwright, off 6 yards, finished a close third in the 100 yards to a winner off 11 yards doing i 9 4-5 secs. Cartwright with 13 yards n the 220 spreadeagled the field and lobbed home easy winner with five yards to spare in 23 2-5 secs. At Wigan the 440 yards looked good on the heat times for W Siddall but an error of judgment left him with the third prize as a consoler. In the 880 yards Arthur Collins, off 66 Nerds, made fair use of his handicap allowance without exhausting his reserve and finishing with rare dash won by two yards in lmin 571-5 secs. Very few of the locals are entering this season and their successes are really remarkable when the training facilities are considered, but despite this and other drawbacks it is getting quite common at many meetings to hear" What is the Warrington man going to do " Well he is generally thereabouts.

TEMPERANCE MEETING.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. GRIFFITHS AND THE BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

There was a good attendance at the weekly meeting in connection with the United Temperance Summer Campaign held at Bridge Foot on Thursday evening. Mr Griffiths presided and the chief speaker was Mr T D Leslie (U.K.A).

The CHAIRMAN said their first work that night was to congratulate the Warrington Board of Guardians who at their last meeting, granted permission to the gentlemen of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union to give illustrative lectures on the misuse of alcohol, to the children in the Cottage Homes at Padgate. That had come about after a great deal of agitation and moral suasion on the part of a few of their friends who were members of the Board of Guardians. One was sorry to find that a simple and desirable question of that kind should have received so much opposition from several of the mem- bers of the Board of Guardians, for so long a time. The matter had been before the Board for years and it had only been at a meeting about a fortnight ago that their friends had persuaded the majority to grant per- mission for such desirable and kindly work to be carried on at Padgate. It had been astonishing to him, that considering the intelligence they wera supposed to have represented in the members of that body, that a a question of that kind should have been opposed so persistently and so long. The question of the evils of alcohol was no longei one for the teetotal faddist alone ; it was lifted abave the mere faddist. Every intelligent man and woman with the least measure of commonsense would admit that alcohol and intemperance were the greatest evils our country was suffering from at the present time. It had baen the one aim and desire of the Band of Hope Union that the children at any rate should have every oppor- tnnity given them to learn about the evils of intemper- ance. The lectures were to be illustrated so that the children would have the facts pointed out to them in the simplest possible way. They heartily rejoiced that after so many years the Guardians had granted their permission. It was the children whom they should educate in the right way and the happy future of the country would be assured. (Applause).

Mr. LESLII. delivered an able address on the Temper- ance question in the course of which he said that the brewing trader was down at the bottom of the list of employment giving trades. He had made that state- ment before and it had been challenged in the press but he could maintain that statement, for it was absolutely true. He did not mean in the distribution of the drink but in the manufacturing of it, it was at the bottom of the list of employment giving trades. In the clothing and millinery trades where a person produced £62 worth of goods one man in the brewing trade could produce £328 worth. So that to produce the same amount in the clothing and millinery trade there would have to be employed four times as many people. They were there not to attack any individuals but the trade as a whole. The drink traffic ASS the greatest moral, physical and political enemy in Warrington and throughout the country. In regard to the moral injury the late president of the divorce court had said that the divorce court could close its doors if it was not for the drink trade. He had realized that practically all the cases coming under his notice were there as a result of intemperance. Had the drink trade nothing to do with the loss in the membership of churches ? Some of the brightest and best lives of the Sunday school had been caught in the snares of this great moral evil. They had only to look in the slums of Warrington and elsewhere to see the degrading influence the drink had on the lives of the children. (Hear, hear.) There was scarcely a family into which the curse bad not entered. It was the greatest physical evil because it affected the brain, and the brain controlled the body. As to its being the greatest political evil in Warrington and the country, he knew something of the evil influence of the drink traffic in the politics of Warrington ; but he would take the question broadly as it affected nearly all the towns in the country. The drink trade had representatives on the Watch Committee which controlled the police, and and it was not a good thing for any town where such a state of affairs existed. The political influence of the Trade was one of the greatest. dangers there was in the country at the present time. Even in the Imperial Parliament many a member was returned by a fuddled and muddled electorate_ Even on the magisterial benches there were representas tives of the liquor traffic. If the drink trade wa removed, at least at election times, representatives would be returned by a sober electorate and there would be more moral force behind elections than at the present time. The sweeping away of drink would remedy the housing problem and would go a long way towards the sweeping away of poverty and unemploy- ment. All those evils were waiting at the door of temperance reform. They could not hope for the closing of all the public houses at once but they wanted them reducing very considerably. A deminution in public houses would mean a diminution in the traffic ; for wherever there was a reduction in facilities there would be a reduction in drinking. (Applause).

SUFFERED 19 YEARS !

MR. ENOCH SANKEY'S AFFAIRS.

Remarkable Disclosures in Bankruptcy.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
PERSONAL EXTRAVAGANCE ADMITTED-

"TOSSING" FOR A MOTOR.

Remarkable disclosures were made at the public examination in bankruptcy at Warrington yesterday, of Mr. Enoch William Sankey, of Heath Farm, Croft, one of the best known horse dealers in the North of England. Considerable interest was centred in the proceedings which took place before Mr. Registrar Ridgway. Mr. Amos (Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.) appeared for the debtor and Mr. T. E. Knowles represented Messrs. Boydell Bros. the peti- tioning creditors. The summary of debtor's statement of affairs showed gross liabilities of 5.-6,797 19s. Bd. There was no estimate c f assets, the deficiency thus being £6,973 19s. Bd. The debtor attributed his failure t "household and personal expenses in excess of income." The official receiver's observa- tions on the case stated : —The debtor who is 57 years of age, states that he commenced business forty years ago at Heath Farm, Croft, near Warrington, as a horse dealer, with £5OO cash, given to him by his mother; when he became of age he received capital of between £6,000 and £7,000 from his father's estate. About twenty years ago he purchased the land and buildings of the farm, together with two other farms, for £7,038. He raised .F.,7,500 on this and other property. He has carried on business as a horse dealer and farmer at Heath Farm and Eaves Brow Farm, Croft, and Cross Lane Farm, Culcheth, up till 15th November, 1911, when he executed a deed of assignment to a trustee for the benefit of his creditors. The unsecured liabilities amounted to £12,878, and the free assets to £l,llB. The latter have been sold for about £9OO. A meeting of creditors was held on 6th December, 1911, and adjourned several times. The chief subject of discussion was a suggested composition of ss. in the A% which was to be adequately guaranteed. A feature was the withdrawal of a considerable section of the creditors. The proposal was abandoned on account of bankruptcy proceedings being threatened by a number of creditors. Two petitions at least have been presented and settled within the last few months, and eventually the petition on which the present proceedings are based was presented. The debtor seems to have carried on an extensive business and earned large profits,but his domestic and personal expenditure has been on a high scale. He estimates that during the last three years the gross profits have amounted to £1,200; in previous years they have ranged from £3,000 to £5,000. Ile states that his expenditure has amounted to £1,500 a year duringt the last three years, but this is probably an under-estimate. The debtor has not kept a cash book or creditors' ledger; he has kept a day book showing his horsedealine• transactions, and a debtors' ledger. Having regard to the extent of his business his book-keeping has been quite inadequate; there is no record of his personal drawings. The debtor states that he first became aware of his insolvency in August, 1911, and that be has contracted debts since then in the hope of being able to sell his farms to advantage, and thus discharge his liabilities. The debtor was questioned at considerable length by Mr Murgatroyd, assistant official receiver. He stated that he commenced business at the age of 1G or 17. His father had then died. . . . .

Did you continue your father's business, or did you start entirely apart from that ?—Entirely apart from it. I lived with my brother. With regard to the estate which came into his hands when be attained his majority, the debtor stated that about £l.OOO of it was in,cash and the remainder con- sisted of cottage property, land and a hotel. Asked how he raised the money to purchase three farms for £7,038 about the year 1892, debtor stated that he paid a deposit and got a mortgage for the rest. You have told us you borrowed £7,500 on mortgage of this property, together with your other property ? Yes.

Does it mean, then, that up to that date your cottage property and public-house property at Croft had not been mortgaged ?—I am not quite sure about that. I am not clear on the point. You ought to know that, Mr Sankey. I am speaking now of 1592 when you bought Heath farm, Cross-lane farm and Eaves-brow farm. Had you mortgages upon the other property which you bad received from your father's estate ?—I think there was a mortgage on the public-house. I am not quite sure. Do you know to what amount ?—No, I don't. Give Mb some idea about the amount ?—I could not give you the amount. Had you, up to that date, sold any portion of the property ?—None. Will you tell me why you had to mortgage that property at all, prior to 1892 ?—I could not tell. Short of money, I suppose. You were short of money then ?—Must have been.

Do I take it from that, that the horse dealing business had not paid even before 1892?—0h, it had paid all right Why were you short of money, if it paid ?—I suppose the expenses beat the business. What kind of expenses ?—The travelling expenses and horse buying. Travelling i expenses, of course, are part of the busi- ness, and so s the horse buying ?—Yes. Hadn't your business paid you up t 011892 ?—I could not really tell you. It is evident that you were short of money and you had bad to mortgage the farm, and yet you say you cannot tell me what amount you received on mortgage? I cannot remember. I have not the slightest idea. Will you tell me this? Was the business a paying business, but did you spend more personally than you ought to have spent ?—That is about it. You are rather guessing at it, I suppose ?—I am quite right. In addition to these farms, haven't you had another farm—New Hey Farm ?—Yes. When was that bought ?—About the year after I bought the others.

Debtor added that the cost of the farm was £4,200, that he supposed he raised the money on mortgage, but he could not tell the amount. Was it £l,OOO or £4,000 ?—I have no idea. Nearer the latter, I should think. What was your object in buying these farms ? Because they were cheap. Were they bought by auction ?—No, privately. Further questioned debtor said he regarded his purchase of New Hey Farm as a bargain. Still you had not money to invest in farms at that time. Did you intend working them ?—I iti,tended working one. The others were tenanted. Have you farmed all these or kept. to Heath Farm ? —I have farmed the others as well. Since when have you farmed them ?—About half-a- dozen years, I think. You have gradually got rid of the tenants and worked them yourself ?—Exactly, yes. The tenants left of their own accord. Debtor stated that he sold New Hey 'Farm in 1903 for £13,000. Did that include the hotel ?—No, just the farm. What did you do with the £13,000 ?—I never handled It at all. It went into the bank after a reduction in mortgage. Were the bank the mortgagees ?—No, some other people. Did the bank get any money out of that £13,000 ? No, they did not. It went to my credit after the mortgagee had taken his share of it. What balance do you think you would have out of it? could not tell you. I never inquired. I don't think it is taxing your memory too much, if you could give some idea. Was it a big amount ? .£3,100 or £4,000. Were you in credit at the bank at that date, in 1803? I was when I had that put to my account. After that amount had been put to, were you still in credit or not ? -About £3,000 in credit I should think. A GOOD BARGAIN.

What did you give for the farm ?—£1,200. You sold it for bow much ?—.£13,000. Then it was a good bargain ?—lt was a good bargain. It would not bejbought as farm land again ?—No, it was bought for Richard Evans and Co. for mining

purposes. - Debtor stated that be sold the hotel for £6,500 about twenty years ago. He did not sell any of the other property left to him by his father. At the time he made the deed of arrangement, Mr J. St. Vigor Fox had tbe first mortgage on the properties. The amount was £7,000 and the 3 per cent. The mortgage bad been on a long time—from the time he purchased Heath Farm. The bank had held a second mortgage since 1903. Your buqines mainly has been horse dealing with, in the last six years' farming.ilas the:farming paid you ? No.

How is that ?—Too much expenses. Has the fact that you have had to be away so much had anything to do with the farming ?—ldon't think so. You have had men who were capable of managing the places ?—Yes. You have had nothing to do with them ?—I think I have lost money on the farms. What about the berse dealing? You have had a great turnover in that ?—Yes. You have been a very extensive buyer and dealer in horses for some years ?—Yes. Has that turnover been profitable to you? I want you to leave out of account anything you have spent

personally. That has nothing to do with the business. Treat it on a business footing entirely.—lt has been profitable. You still say the farms have not paid you, apart from anything you have withdrawn from them for personal expenditnre ?—Exactly. But the horse dealing you ray has paid you ?—Yes. Has it paid you well ?—Yes. What number of horses have you turned over in a year ?-1 could not say. No idea ?—A thousand, I should think. Good priced horses, were they ?—Yes. To what figure would you go ?—£loo or £BO. Have you sold them mainly to public bodies such as railway companies ?—Yes, and private customers. But mainly to railway companies ?—Principally, yes I take it that the business is a cash business, isn't it ? —Cash business.

It must be cash, as far as:pur,haser gees, I suppose? —Absolutely. You turn them over very quickly don't you 2—Yes. Sometimes you have them on your hands a long time. Generally s waking, have you turned over your pur- chases pretty quickly ?—Generally speaking, I have. No bad debts?—Occasionally. I have been let in for a few hundreds at times.

Have you any idea %hat your turnover has amounted tomn cash ?-1 have'nt.

Does the hana account represent pretty well what your turrover has been ?—Yes.

_ Have you paii pretty well most of your receipts into the bank ?—I have.

And paid moat of your acconnts by cheque ?—I have. Then the bank account is a fair criterion of the turn- over ?—Yes.

In 1907 you paid into the bank £27,903. Was that a good year ?—No, I don't think so.

A TURNOVER or £50,00. What do you think would be a good year ?—I think I have turned £50,000 over —more. Would £50,000 pay you ?—Yes. Handsomely, would it not ?—lt would pay well. Would £27,000 ?—Yes, that would pay me if all was straight sailing. In 1908 when your payments into the bank dropped to £15,524, would you call that a profitable year or not ? —No ; losing money. That is making a profit out of buying and selling, hut losing money on personal and household expenses ? Exactly.

Debtor could offer no explanation RR to why the amount paid into the bank dropped from .£27,000 in 1907 to £15,000 in 1908. He had no idea it had dropped so much as that.

Does that mean you never saw your pass book ?—I never kept a record of the money I paid into the bank. When did you tee sour pass book ?—I never did t.ee it, practically. Never saw the pass book ?—Very seldom. I paid money in and never had a receipt for it or anything. Weren't you interested in knowing how the balance was at the bank ?—Certainly. Idid inquire how the balance was but I didn't look at the pass book. I left it there for months. In 1909 the turnover went up to £20,492.. Is there any reason why it should have done ?—No. In 1910 it dropped to .011,161. That is what you would call a bad year, I suppose ?—Yes. And in 1911 it dropped to .£10,504—a worse year. Can you explain the drop in your turnover from 1909? —I must not have had the horses. The capital must have been going. _

Do you mean you found yourself crippled in buying? —I did. Am, ...I Had you met with any difficulty in buying horses by reason of not being able to pay for them during those years ?—yes.

Replying to further questions, debtor said that in 1910 the bank declined to allow him to draw beyond a certain figure. When he gave the bank a second mort- gage in 1908, he had an overdraft of £6,500 which was practically unsecured. He himself insisted upon giving the second mortgage. There was no mention of a definite amount, and he did not think the bank treated him quite fairly over the business. At the time he made the deed of arrangement, the overdraft stood at £6,000 or £7,000. _

Cap'ital is, of course, quite necessary in business. But taking your own case, you were buying and selling for cash. You would not need a deal of capital unless you were spending it elsewhere, would you ?—I have no private outlet for it, It was all spent is the busi- ness and expenses. 1 You seriously ssy that the drop in the turnover is caused by lack of cspital in the last two years ?—That is right. Debtor said he kept a " horse book " in which he entered the purchases and sales of horses You have kept that accurately ?—Not cite, I don't think. I have perhaps been a little bit negligent. Since when ?—This last year or two. What have you omitted to enter? Purchases of houses ?—No. I dare say it is practically allright. Both for purchases and sales ?—I think so, yes. But not what you might term accurately. Do you mean the entries in it are not accurate,or that you omitted to put some down ?—I mean the perhaphs sales are not accurate.

You omitted i'o-p-u't—the sales in, you mean ?—Perhaps so, yes. I always put the purchases down. Apart from that you have not any record of cash received or paid 7—No. Except what appears in the bank book ?—That is all. Is that the only book of account you have kept?—Yes. Debtor added that he believed his son kept a record of the income and the wages paid at the farms. Have you made out any balance sheets at all ?—No. No trading accounts made out to show what you were making in one year ?—No. Questioned as to his gross profit on the sale of horss during the past three years, debtor put it down at about £l,OOO or £1,500 a year. What, does it cost you in travelling expenses ?—That I cannot tell you. Roughly ?—£2o or £3O a week. Are you away most of the year ?—Yes, always away. Is not £2O or £3O a week rather a liberal estimate ? No. I think it is a fair estimate:.

Debtor further stated tbat his gross profits had reached as much as £4,000 or £5,000 a year. When did you first flnd out that you were insolvent ? —I never did realise it until last summer.

_ How dui you find it out then ?—Pressure from the bank.

What effect bad that upon you ?—I could not buy horses.

What did you do—give up buying ?—No. I had to buy and do the best I could. I had to raise money somenow Whom did you raise it from ?—Different people. Private loans, you mean ?—Yes. How long have you been borrowing money privately? —From friends, do you mean ? Yes. How many veers ?—A good many. Twenty years ?—Oetting on for it. Why didn't you stop last summer ?—I thought I was going to sell a farm. I was in treaty. Had you ever thought of selling the farms before ? Yes.

Had vou ever been in treaty before ?—Yes. Had it always fallen through ?—Yes. Had you any particular reason to think it would come off last summer ?—Yes. Were you thinking of getting more than agricultural? value for it ?—Yes.

Was it a case of coal again ?—Yes. had you any offer at all ?—Not a fast offer, but I thought it was as good as sold.

Apart from any expectations from the farms, did you know before last summer that you owed a great deal more than you were worth ?—I never went into the business at all. You most have done something ?-1 never went into the accounts. You always knew pretty wsll what you owed the bank, did'nt you ?—I was careless. Yes, but carelessness does not excuse it altogether —No.

_ It could not be carelessness to the extent of not knowing what you owed ?—Quite so. When the deed of assignment was being dealt with you owed £ll,OOO ?—Yes. You must have known before last summer that you owed a large amount of money ?—Ves, but I was expecting to recover. I put it to you that apart from the expectation of selling the farm, you have known for a few years that you were insolvent ?—lt is quite true. It all depended upon selling this farm for an amount exceeding agricultural value ?—And business being better.

But business was dropping, you see, for the last two years. You nay you were crippled for money ?—Yes, quite true.

TRADV.S.MEN'S CLAIMS. Amongst your unsecured liabilities are certain private accounts. Take a claim here from Messrs Boydell Bros. for goods supplied since January 11th, 1909, £227. You (illy paid off £75 on account of that. Why should not you have paid them more ?—I suppose I hadn't got it. But you were turning over a considerable amount of money. Can you give roe some idea ?—.£l. or £2 a week, 1 dare say. Are they married ?—No, they are only boys of 20 and 21.

or .£2 a we k for three sons, does add up in a year's time, doesn't it, ?—quite true. Were they earning it ?—Yes, I mean to say their expenses when they went away buying. They had DO axed wage. I never gave It consideration.

- ABSOLUTELY CARELESS." But if yen paid accounts through the bank you must have seen that the expenses were heavy, mustn't you?— I never looked at the expenses. What have you looked at, Mr Sankey, in the last two or three years ?—Doing the buskins. I never took expenses into consideration at all. I have been care- less about it altogether. Careless in everything ?—Absolutely careless. And extravagant ?—No, I don't mean it that way. I have not been careless in buying or selling. I mean to say I have never taken expenses into consideration as I ought to have done. You mean business expenses?—Exactly. What about household expenses ?—They have been all right. They have not been excessive ?—I don't think so. You think 1:1,500 a year is not too much ?—Ne, I don't think so.

A reasonable amount do you think ?—A reasonable amount I think, £l,OOO or £1,500. Doesn't it rather depend upon what were making making ?—Exactly. Well .what were you making ?—Then I was making plenty of money. In the last two or three years ?—Well the expenses have not been as much in the last two or three years. Are you sure they haven't ?—I think so. They could not have been.

Have they been £l,OOO ?—Perhaps so, Have you been making that in profit ?—I don't think I have.

Further questioned the debtor said Mr Ball bad lent him money at various times and had never been paid any interest since 1895. How is that ?—He never asked me. I never paid aoy interest.

Were these loans or gifts ?—Loans,l suppose. Did you give promissory notes for them ?—I did. For each loan ?—No, not for each. Then it was really a loan from your son to yourself ? Yes.

Did you wear it as recently as Sunday, June oth ?—I dare say I did. I wore it yesterday. Further questioned, the debtor said he had expected to make £15,000 profit on the sale of the farm. He further agreed that he must have been insolvent without knowing it. You admit that you have been pressed by various creditors within recent years ?—Yes. I take it you have received accounts from them ?—I have. Have you paid attention to those accouncts?—None whatever.

Did not the constant receipt of these accounts suggest to you that you were unable to pay your debts? —1 did not trouble about them at all, I am sorry to say. You simply ignored them. Had you any reason whatever to think you were insolvent?—None whatever, Perhaps you did not think at all ?—I did not. With regard to the books of acconnt, I suggest that a man who was carrying on an extensive horse dealing business would find books of account an essential?— Yes. I ought to have kept books, but I didn't. Debtor admitted that he produced a couple of cheques, which were made payable to him, when he visited Messrs. Boydell in November last. He had not received cash for the cheques, but his object in show- ing them was not to induce the firm to refrain from pressing him. He simply wanted them to know his position—that he could not get money on the cheques. The examination was adjourned until August 2nd, the Registrar making an order for the debtor to furnish fuller particulars of his affairs.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 6, 1912.

WLEY BRIDGE IN THE MAKING.

THE OLD ENGLISH FAIR.

The delights of the Old English Fair, in aid of the Million Penny Fund, should draw huge crowds to Victoria Park on Thursday, Friday and Saturday next. The various committees have been bard at work arranging a host of attractions which will vie with anything of the kind previously seen in War- rington and only a spell of real summer weather is necessary to crown their labours with success. The opening ceremonies will be performed at 2.30 each day as follows : —Thurday, Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. ; Friday, Mrs. A. H. Crosfield ; Saturday, Mrs. Ziba Armitage. The amusements will include all the traditional features of the fairground, and in addition there will be entertainments by the Park Pierrots —a company of well known local artistes. Dancing on the green will afford pleasure to many each evening, the bands which have promised their services being Crosfield's, the Pioneer, Blue Coat Szhool and Padgate Industrial School. A rifle range will be under the superintendence of the L. & N.-W. Railway Institute Rifle Club, and vaiious shooting competitions have been arranged. Friday will be a day of special interest, as Mr. W. H. Ewen, the renowned aviator, will arrive at the Park at 5 p.m. in his Caudron biplane. The machine will be on view in an enclosure on the ground at an extra charge. An " terial post " will also be estab- lished, and it is expected that most visitors to the fair will avail themselves of this novel means of sending missives to their friends. To-day a shop will be opened at Market-gate' for the sale of postcards and programmes. Dolls sent in for competition will also be on view.

===== 0002601_19120706_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
SMOKE.

THE MAYOR'S VIEW ASSAILED.

Dangerous Gases in the Air.

A correspondent calls our attention to the statement made by Dr. Joseph, the Mayor, in the discussion in the Council on the smoke nuisance, that it is not desirable to discourage smoke on the ground that the sulphuric acid produced is a valuable disinfectant and speci- ally useful in killing the germs of diphtheria. " But in this " asks our correspondent, " is not the worthy Doctor overlooking a fact which destroys the premises for his conclu- sion? The amount of sulphuric acid evolved with the smoke is no evidence of imperfect combustion, and has practically nothing to do with it. The amount of sulphuric acid pro- duced is entirely dependent upon the amount of sulphur in the coal, and whether the coal is burnt extravagantly or economic- ally the amount of sulphur, and hence the after-product sulphuric acid, is the same. We would refer the Mayor to the opinion of the Medical Officer of Health for Manchester, James Niven M.B. LL.D. who is an acknow- ledged authority, and who attributes most of the irritation, pulmonary congestion and high death rate in periods of fog to the acids which attach themselves to the moisture and the soot in the atmosphere that is breathed. A further health point we think the doctor has overlooked is the production through imperfect combustion of carbon-monoxide, which is a poisonous gas with neither taste nor smell. When combustion is perfect, carbon-dioxide (carbonic acid gas)is produced instead of the gas which is deleterious to health. The CO2 is a comparatively innocent gas; we inhale it everytime we drink aerated waters. It is objectionable only in so far as it displaces pure air. "We would respectively suggest to the worthy Doctor that he should study this question a little more closely. His position and profession add great weight to his pro- nouncements on such a question, and it is very desirable that they should be accurate when he deals with scientific conditions affecting health. For a gentleman of the medical profession to defend the production of smoke from a health point of view for a reason which does not exist; for him to ignore the dissemination over the town of soot and grit and of a highly poisonous gas; to ignore the evil effects of air contamina- tion when there are so many in the town suffering from chest, throat, and lung troubles; to excuse a practice which spreads dirt all around, checks vegetable and animal life and lessens sunlight, all for a reason that has not scientific foundation—seems to us very surprising. "As a member of an important profession, as a painstaking and capable Mayor, and as a worthy citizen and gentleman, we have for Dr. Joseph a sincere respect, but we are con- fident in this matter he is taking a wrong line, and one that will have a serious effect upon the health of the people, and the cleanli- ness and brightness of the town over which he presides. What action is likely to be taken by the Health Committee, or how can the Inspectors be expected to do their part with zeal when it is known that the official head is opposed, or at least indifferent, to smoke prevention, and appears almost to go out of his way to find excuses for the nuisance? And again, what effect is it likely to have upon the manufacturers to know, or have reason to suppose, that the first citizen of the borough,who is a medical man, appears to be of the opinion from a health point of view that the more smoke they fail to consume and turn into the air the better?

===== 0002601_19120706_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TIME LIMIT.

Who is to Blame—Council or Health Committee? To THE EDITOR "EXAMINER."

Sir,—During the last few weeks your correspondents in the " Examiner " have said some very strong things about . the Warrington smoke nuisance, especially alleging incapacity on the part of the Health Committee. But to affect any good purpose it is necessary to get into closer touch with facts. The most important of these, and one which so far has not received attention, is the recommendation of the Health Com- mittee, approved by the Council I think in the year 1908, that the time limit for the emission of black smoke be extended from three minutes to five minutes for the half hour,with three minutes continuous emission This is a crucial point, and in fairness to the Health Committee, the question should .be argued from this standpoint. If we start with the presumption that a five minutes limit can be justified under present conditions and knowledge of boiler firing, then probably little fault can fairly.be found with the Health Committee. The .real question is: Was the Town Council justified in approving the recommendation to , extend the limit from three to five minutes? It would be interesting to know on what ground this was done. Presumedly informa- tion was got from other towns in order to see how the Warrington limit compared. If we take the towns with limits for the half-hour, or hour,and reduce all to the half by dividing the hour limit by two, we find that the average limit at the time the Warrington limit was changed from three to five was three minutes thirteen seconds. There does not appear, on the surface, any reason why the Warrington limit should Have been extended. When we find such towns as Manchester, Salford, Oldham, Bolton, Bury, Cardiff, Glasgow, Newcastle, &c., are working with a limit of three minutes or under for the half hour, and that the manufacturers in these towns can adapt themselves to the requirements and do it with advantage to themselves, it is strange that the Warrington Council should have gone out of their way to practically double the Warrington limit. Those who are obliged to live in the town, and who cannot afford to find relief by removal to the suburbs are entitled to some explanation from the Health Committee why they raised the time limit to a point at which it is almost useless as.a restraint to careless firing, inadequate boiler capacity, and consequent waste of fuel. FAIR PLAY.

EDUCATION ACT, 1902. CHESHIRE LOCAL EDUCATION AUTHORITY. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN in accordance with the provisions of Section 8 (1) and (2) of the Education Act, 1902, that we, the undersigned, pro- pose to enlarge the Halton Grammar School, situated in the township of 'Halton, in the Rural District of Runcorn, in the County of Chester, by providing additional accommodation for about 140 children. The Board of Education consider this enlargement to be such as to amount to the provision of a new School. The said School is conducted as a Voluntary School or School not provided by the Local Education Authority under the conditions prescribed in Section 7of the said Act, and religious instruction is given in school hours in accordance with the provisions of Section 7 (2) of the Elementary Education Act 1870, and in conformity with the doctrines of the Church of England. The School will ne available for the follow- ing area:—The TOWNSHIPS OF HALTON, NORTON, STOCKHAM, and CLIFTON, in the RURAL DISTRICT OF RUNCORN. The Managers of any existing School, or the Local Education Authority, or any ten ratepayers in the area for which it is proposed to provide the School, may appeal against this proposal by a letter addressed to the Secretary, Board of Education, Whitehall, London, S.W., which should reach him within three months from the date of this notice. R. M. BROOKE, C. L. EVANS,

--- OVER SIXTY APPLICATIONS FOR PADGATE APPOINTMENTS. The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr E L Bent presiding. There were also present : Miss Broadbent, Dr Murray, Revs T It Matthews and A M Mitchell, Messrs A J Jolley (deputy-chairman), Billinee, W Gill, NV C Brooks, R Lee, W Houghton, W Flanagan, J A Hughes, 'AI W Seton, A Brooks, J A Rushworth, A Botromley (clerk), R I Partridge (assistant clerk), NV J Brown (master), and A Wright (superintendent of Cottage Homes). THE COTTAGE HOME'. REV. A. M. MITCHELL AND THE NEW APPOINTMENTS. At a meeting of the Cottage Homes Committee, Mr A Wright was appointed schoolmaster and superin- tendent pro tent until his successor takes up duty. The CLERK reported that he had received 63 applica- tions for the joint appointment of schoolmaster and superintendent and matron of the Cottige Homes. ' The applications were referred to the Cottage Homes , Committee. The Rev A H MITCHELL asked if there would be any advantage in having the offices separated and unmarried candidates appointed ? Would it work out any cheaper in the end as regarded rations ? The CHAIRMAN: I don't think it would be advisable. The Rev A M MITCHELL: Let us understand how to view the matter. Is there any advantage in having a married couple, compared with the other arrange- rnent ? The CLERK Yes, because you have apartments there for a married couple. The Board, if they desired, could consider applications from couples, who, if appointed, would be married. THE TEMPERANCE LECTURE. The CLERK read a letter from Mr Chandos secretary of the Lancashire and Cheshire Hand of Hope Union, who stated that Dr J Morris had arranged with the superintendent of the Cottage Homes to give an illustrated lesson based on the Board of Education's syllabus on temperance in the Cottage Homes on August 29th at 2 p.m. The intimation was given in order that those members of the Board who desired cauld be present. Mr HouoirTorr : That means the children will lose half-a-day's school instruction. Mr A BROOKS : They will get something better than that.

INHUMAN FATHER SENT TO PRISON. At St. Helens on Tuesday, Thomas Scott, plasterer, of 99, Oldfield-street, was prosecuted by the National Society, for neglect of children.—Mr Warner, jun., said that Mrs Scott was • suffering frbm phthisis, hut her husband was drunken and lazy. He was a plasterer, and could earn good wages. On Saturday last witness went to the house and found it in a destitute condition. There was not a particle of food, and all the family slept on one bed. The two elder boys (seventeen and fourteen) who had been working all night, were laid asleep on this bed. The two younger children were in a wretched state. In March last he warned Scott that he would be prosecuted if he did not treat his family better. Mr Cottom, relieving officer, said he had several times relieved this family on account of the man's neglect and failure to work. The Cbairman (after looking at prisoner's record) : I am afraid you are not only a wastrel, but a bully You will have to go to gaol for six months' hard labour • COMMERCIAL PAlLllRMS.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the number of Commercial Failures published in Great Britain and Ireland during the quarter ending Saturday, June 29th, was 1,981.
